RAPTOR-GEN: RA pid P os T eri OR GEN erator for Bayesian Learning in Biomanufacturing
Wandi Xu, Wei Xie

Abstract
Biopharmaceutical manufacturing is vital to public health but lacks the agility for rapid, on-demand production of biotherapeutics due to the complexity and variability of bioprocesses. To overcome this, we introduce RApid PosTeriOR GENerator (RAPTOR-GEN), a mechanism-informed Bayesian learning framework designed to accelerate intelligent digital twin development from sparse and heterogeneous experimental data. This framework is built on a multi-scale probabilistic knowledge graph (pKG), formulated as a stochastic differential equation (SDE)-based foundational model that captures the nonlinear dynamics of bioprocesses. RAPTOR-GEN consists of two ingredients: (i) an interpretable metamodel integrating linear noise approximation (LNA) that exploits the structural information of bioprocessing mechanisms and a sequential learning strategy to fuse heterogeneous and sparse data, enabling inference of latent state variables and explicit approximation of the intractable likelihood function; and (ii) an efficient Bayesian posterior sampling method that utilizes Langevin diffusion (LD) to accelerate posterior exploration by exploiting the gradients of the derived likelihood. It generalizes the LNA approach to circumvent the challenge of step size selection, facilitating robust learning of mechanistic parameters with provable finite-sample performance guarantees. We develop a fast and robust RAPTOR-GEN algorithm with controllable error. Numerical experiments demonstrate its effectiveness in uncovering the underlying regulatory mechanisms of biomanufacturing processes.

1
Introduction

Although the biopharmaceutical industry has developed various innovative biopharmaceuticals for severe diseases such as cancer and COVID-19, the analytical tests required by biopharmaceuticals of complex molecular structure are time-consuming and expensive. Also, as new biotherapeutics (e.g., cell and gene therapies) become more and more personalized, tailoring decisions to cells and molecules is critical to mitigating stresses and supporting a successful biomanufacturing process. However, low productivity, low flexibility, high variability, and human errors, which alone account for 80% of out-of-specification variations
(
Cintron 2015
)
, make rapid production of new and existing biotherapeutics on demand an ever-increasing challenge for current biomanufacturing systems. It is urgent to accelerate the development of flexible, automatic, and robust biomanufacturing systems.

Unlike chemically synthesized small-molecule drugs, biopharmaceuticals are manufactured using living organisms (e.g., cells) as production factories. Fundamentally, their manufacturing processes constitute biological systems-of-systems (Bio-SoS), characterized by dynamic interactions among hundreds of biological, physical, and chemical factors across molecular, cellular, and macroscopic scales. These multi-scale interactions collectively influence product yield and critical quality attributes. To model the complexity and nonlinear dynamics of Bio-SoS, we introduce a general multi-scale probabilistic knowledge graph (pKG) as a mechanistic framework. The pKG is formulated using continuous-time stochastic differential equations (SDEs), enabling the integration of sparse and heterogeneous data from diverse sources that range from single-cell measurements of gene/protein expression to bulk concentrations of nutrients and metabolites in bioreactors. Based on observations from partially observed states, the pKG can function as a soft sensor to track critical latent variables, support optimal control strategies, and facilitate real-time product release. Its modular design organizes multi-scale models into reusable components, allowing flexible assembly of digital twins tailored to specific manufacturing systems. For example,
Zheng et al. 2024
demonstrates its application to monolayer and aggregate cultures of induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSCs). This modularity also supports system integration across manufacturing platforms and provides a foundation for scalable biofoundry development
(
Hillson et al. 2019
)
.

This paper focuses on the foundational building block of multi-scale mechanistic models in bioprocesses, i.e., the stochastic reaction network (SRN). A SRN models the interactions among molecular species through chemical reactions, capturing the causal dependencies that connect molecular regulatory mechanisms to the emergent dynamics and variability of macroscopic biomanufacturing systems.
More specifically, SRNs model bioprocess dynamics using a stochastic mechanistic framework that defines how reaction rates depend on random states such as species concentrations and environmental conditions. For instance, environmental factors such as pH, temperature, and ion concentrations influence biomolecular structure and function (e.g., enzyme activity), as well as molecular interactions (e.g., collisions and binding events), thereby shaping the regulatory behavior of the SRN. The functional form of the reaction rates, which encodes the bioprocess regulatory mechanisms, is assumed to be known and grounded in established domain knowledge. Common examples include Michaelis-Menten kinetics and empirical Monod-type models, which describe the logic of molecular interactions and enzymatic reaction rates
(
Kyriakopoulos et al. 2018
)
. However, the mechanistic parameters that govern these regulatory processes are typically unknown and must be inferred.

In this paper, we introduce an innovative sequential Bayesian inference on the pKG mechanistic model in SDE form to quickly fuse heterogeneous data and generate posterior samples of unknown parameters quantifying model uncertainty.
This provides a coherent way to pool information from the structure of bioprocessing mechanisms and sparse heterogeneous data, advancing scientific understanding of the underlying regulatory mechanisms and supporting the development of intelligent digital twins to facilitate flexible, automatic, and robust biomanufacturing.

Due to the limited number of experimental runs in biomanufacturing
(
Wang et al. 2024a
)
, combined with its high intrinsic stochasticity, the model uncertainty is typically substantial. Since frequentist approaches rely on asymptotic approximations, they may be less reliable under such conditions. Consequently, Bayesian inference, particularly posterior sampling methods, is often preferred for mechanistic model inference, as it better accommodates uncertainty and limited data
(
Beaumont et al. 2002
,
Toni et al. 2009
,
Csilléry et al. 2010
,
Ross et al. 2017
)
. These studies can be broadly categorized into two branches, i.e., likelihood-based and likelihood-free approaches. Likelihood-based posterior sampling methods rely on the closed-form expression of the likelihood function to construct stochastic inference procedures, typically involving Markov chains in the parameter space. Examples include Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)
(
Hastings 1970
,
Geman and Geman 1984
)
, sequential importance sampling, and sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) methods
(
Del Moral et al. 2006
)
. In contrast, likelihood-free methods bypass explicit likelihood evaluation by leveraging Monte Carlo simulations of SRNs. These methods either embed simulation within inference algorithms, such as particle MCMC
(
Andrieu et al. 2010
)
, or generate sample paths to assess the similarity between the simulated data and the experimental observations; notable examples include approximate Bayesian computation (ABC)
(
Blum 2010
,
Sisson et al. 2018
)
and its variants, such as ABC-MCMC
(
Marjoram et al. 2003
)
and ABC-SMC
(
Sisson et al. 2007
,
Beaumont et al. 2009
,
Xie et al. 2022b
)
.

The complicated nonlinear structure and high stochasticity of SRNs make simulations generating a large amount of sample paths computationally expensive, resulting in a prohibitive computational burden of likelihood-free posterior sampling methods. Likelihood-based posterior sampling methods can benefit from incorporating the structural information of SRNs provided in the closed form of approximate likelihood. However, SRN has various key features that make it difficult to obtain the explicit likelihood approximation. First, the continuous-time state transition model is highly nonlinear. At any time, the reaction rates that characterize the regulatory mechanisms of SRNs are functions of the random state. This means that the state transition model has double stochasticity, making it analytically intractable to get the closed-form likelihood. Second, since the state is often partially observed, we need to integrate out the unobserved state variables to get the likelihood. Third, data collected from biomanufacturing processes are heterogeneous and subject to measurement errors. In addition, the model posterior inference space is large, as biopharmaceutical manufacturing processes have high complexity, high stochasticity, and limited data
(
Xie et al. 2022a
)
. The effectiveness of Metropolis-based MCMC approaches and sequential importance sampling depends heavily on the selection of good proposal distributions, which is challenging due to complex dependent effects of mechanistic parameters on bioprocess dynamics and variations.

To enable explicit likelihood approximation and improve the efficiency of likelihood-based posterior sampling, we propose a novel sequential Bayesian learning framework built on the pKG, named RApid PosTeriOR GENerator (RAPTOR-GEN). This framework is designed to rapidly integrate sparse and heterogeneous data, infer mechanistic parameters (or bioprocess regulatory mechanisms), and track latent states. RAPTOR-GEN consists of two main components and follows a general learning scheme for SDEs, leveraging both linear noise approximation (LNA) and sequential learning to improve scalability and inference accuracy.

The first component of the framework constructs an interpretable dynamic metamodel, termed pKG-LNA, by applying LNA to the SDE-based pKG. This metamodel serves as a surrogate for the state transition densities of the underlying SDEs and enables inference of heterogeneous latent states, thereby allowing closed-form approximation of otherwise intractable likelihoods. To mitigate the approximation error inherent in naive LNA, which tends to accumulate over time relative to state transitions, we enhance the metamodel through sequential learning and dynamic knowledge updates on the pKG. Specifically, we perform Bayesian updates of inferred state variables at each observation time point using partially observed and noisy data (see Section
4
for details). Compared to existing black-box metamodels such as Gaussian processes (GPs), the sequential pKG-LNA metamodel retains the advantage of tractable conditional distributions. That is, it provides explicit expressions for the conditional distributions of latent state variables at any time, capturing the interdependencies among state components. Unlike GPs, however, pKG-LNA fully leverages the structural information encoded in the Bio-SoS mechanistic model in SDE form. This allows for improved sample efficiency, enhanced interpretability of predictions, and seamless integration of data across diverse biomanufacturing processes.

The second component of the proposed framework is a Bayesian posterior sampling method that fully exploits the derived likelihood. This method is inspired by Langevin diffusion (LD), a concept from physics that models the stochastic evolution of particles or molecules under the influence of a potential function representing a force field, combined with a Brownian motion term to account for thermodynamic fluctuations. In the context of Bayesian inference, the potential function is replaced by the target posterior distribution. A key property of LD is that, under appropriate conditions, it admits the target posterior as its unique stationary distribution. This allows the gradient of the derived likelihood, reflecting the structure of bioprocess regulatory mechanisms and the causal interdependence of the mechanistic parameters, to guide the posterior search toward regions of high likelihood, thereby accelerating convergence in MCMC sampling. However, numerical discretization of the LD-based SDE introduces bias in the posterior sampling process. A large step size facilitates rapid exploration of the parameter space but increases bias, while a small step size reduces bias but requires many iterations, especially problematic in high-dimensional settings. Even with a Metropolis-Hastings correction at each step, selecting an appropriate step size remains challenging
(
Biron-Lattes et al. 2024
)
. To address this, we generalize the concept of LNA to LD in the limit, enabling approximation of the stationary distribution without discretizing the LD. This approach eliminates the need for step size tuning and improves computational efficiency. LNA, based on the Taylor expansion of SDEs, also allows us to quantify the approximation error as a function of the data size (see Section
5
for details).

This study fundamentally combines science, uncertainty, and general intelligence in a new unified statistical learning framework specific to Bio-SoS. We summarize the key contributions as follows.

•

We propose a computationally efficient Bayesian learning framework for SRN, termed RAPTOR-GEN, which consists of two main components: (i) Bayesian updating pKG-LNA, an interpretable metamodel built on an SDE-based mechanistic model, combined with sequential learning, to fuse partially observed and noisy heterogeneous measurements. This enables a closed-form approximation of otherwise intractable likelihoods. (ii) LD-LNA posterior sampling, a novel Bayesian sampling method that leverages gradient information from the derived likelihood. Inspired by LD and LNA, it efficiently learns mechanistic parameters and quantifies model uncertainty without requiring discretization.

•

We show that, under appropriate conditions, the probability distribution of random samples generated by LD-LNA converges to the target posterior distribution. Furthermore, we provide a quantitative bound, expressed as a function of the data size of real-world observations, to evaluate the finite-sample performance of the posterior approximation obtained via LD-LNA. As the data size increases toward infinity, the approximate posterior satisfies the well-known Bernstein–von Mises (BvM) theorem, establishing a formal connection between Bayesian inference and frequentist large-sample theory.

•

To ensure the applicability of RAPTOR-GEN in challenging scenarios such as sparse data collection and high-dimensional parameter spaces, we develop a dedicated algorithm for fast and robust posterior sampling of inferred parameters. Unlike traditional MCMC-type algorithms, which often suffer from convergence uncertainty during sampling, the proposed algorithm is deterministic, offering more stable, reliable, and reproducible performance. We further analyze the algorithm’s convergence properties and show that its approximation error is controllable, providing theoretical guarantees for its accuracy and robustness.

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. Section
2
reviews the related studies in the literature. Section
3
introduces the problem formulation, including the SDE-based pKG mechanistic model for SRNs. Sections
4
and
5
present the core components of the proposed Bayesian learning framework, RAPTOR-GEN. Specifically, Section
4
develops the LNA-based metamodel for pKG, enhanced through sequential learning, and Section
5
introduces the LD-LNA Bayesian inference method and analyzes its finite-sample and asymptotic performance. Section
6
describes the implementation procedure for RAPTOR-GEN and investigates its convergence behavior. Section
7
presents empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework. Section
8
concludes the paper and outlines future research directions.

2
Literature Review

Operations Research and Management in Biomanufacturing.
Existing OR/OM approaches
(
Peroni et al. 2005
,
Liu et al. 2013
,
Martagan et al. 2020
,
Zheng et al. 2021
,
Wang et al. 2024a
)
typically ignore the fundamental mechanisms of biomanufacturing processes, which limits their sample efficiency, performance, and interpretability. The previous work proposes a model-based reinforcement learning (RL) framework built on a dynamic Bayesian network-based pKG model that takes into account both inherent stochasticity and model uncertainty of biomanufacturing systems
(
Zheng et al. 2023b
)
. However, the pKG used in this RL framework only considers a simplified linear state transition model, which cannot faithfully represent the underlying mechanisms and capture the key features of biomanufacturing processes including nonlinear and nonstationary dynamics, partially observed states, and double stochasticity.

The Bayesian learning framework proposed in this paper on the SDE-based pKG mechanistic model with a modular design, characterizing the fundamental mechanisms of biomanufacturing systems, especially molecular interactions, addresses the key limitation of existing OR/OM studies. This enables us to fuse sparse and heterogeneous data collected from different production processes, support sample-efficient and interpretable learning, facilitate manufacturing systems integration, and accelerate the development of flexible, automatic, and robust biomanufacturing systems.

Linear Noise Approximation (LNA).
LNA was originally proposed to approximate the solution of chemical master equations, which are forward Kolmogorov equations represented by a system of coupled SDEs that describe the time evolution of the probability distribution of molecular counts
(
McQuarrie 1967
,
Gillespie 1992
)
. LNA simplifies the solution of these complex SDEs by applying a Taylor expansion, reducing the problem to solving a set of relatively simple ordinary differential equations (ODEs). Beyond its application in biochemical systems, LNA is closely related to diffusion approximation techniques used in steady-state analysis of various stochastic systems, such as call centers
(
Mandelbaum and Zeltyn 2009
)
and hospital operations
(
Feng and Shi 2018
)
. These methods approximate the stationary distributions of Markov chains using expansions of the Markov chain generator, incorporating first-, second-, and higher-order terms
(
Braverman et al. 2024
)
. In this broader context, LNA can be viewed as a special case of diffusion approximations, tailored specifically to bioprocessing and biomanufacturing systems. In particular, within Bayesian inference for SRNs, LNA has been employed to address the challenge of intractable likelihood functions, enabling efficient inference
(
Ruttor and Opper 2009
,
Fearnhead et al. 2014
)
.

This paper is the first generalization of LNA to Bayesian sequential learning on the pKG, marking a significant and novel contribution to the learning of Bio-SoS mechanisms. We enhance LNA through sequential learning on the pKG to reduce the likelihood approximation error, and further generalize LNA to approximate the LD-based posterior sampling process with controllable approximation error. This generalization is nontrivial. The classical LNA assumes the presence of a size parameter in biomanufacturing systems, such as the volume of a bioreactor, under which the stationary distribution of molecular concentrations converges to the LNA as the size parameter tends to infinity
(
Grunberg and Del Vecchio 2023
)
. Analogously, when generalizing LNA to Bayesian posterior sampling, we observe that as the data size increases, fluctuations in parameter estimates diminish, similar to how molecular fluctuations vanish in large-volume systems. However, unlike the explicit relationship between molecular concentrations and system volume, the connection between parameter estimation and data size is implicitly encoded in the posterior distribution. This implicitness poses a challenge in deriving quantitative bounds that explicitly characterize the dependence on data size, which are essential for assessing the finite-sample performance of the LNA approximation in posterior sampling. To address this, we derive a theoretical bound quantifying the impact of data size, sparsity, and parameter dimensionality on the convergence rate of the LNA approximation.
This result provides new insights into the scalability and accuracy of LNA-based methods in Bayesian inference for complex stochastic systems.

Langevin Diffusion (LD) and Its Role in Generative AI.
LD is closely connected to the rapidly evolving field of generative AI, particularly diffusion-based models
(
Ho et al. 2020
,
Bunne et al. 2024
)
. These models operate by progressively corrupting training data with increasing levels of noise during a forward diffusion process, and then learning to reverse this corruption to generate structured outputs, such as images or text, via a reverse process that emulates physical diffusion dynamics. LD plays a central role in the reverse sampling procedure of many generative models; notable examples include denoising diffusion probability models (DDPM)
(
Ho et al. 2020
)
, scoring matching with Langevin dynamics (SMLD)
(
Song and Ermon 2019
)
, and scoring-based generation models with SDEs
(
Song et al. 2021
)
. In contrast to these existing approaches, our work diverges from the standard generative AI paradigm. Specifically, we do not train surrogate models, such as denoising neural networks, to predict noise, nor do we directly apply discretized LD for sampling. Instead, we leverage the structural information embedded in the pKG mechanistic foundational model for Bio-SoS systems to develop a novel Bayesian inference framework. This approach also bypasses the need for discretization of LD in a posterior search, thereby accelerating the inference process while maintaining theoretical rigor.

3
Problem Statement

Section
3.1
formulates the SDE-based pKG mechanistic model of SRN. Section
3.2
introduces likelihood-based Bayesian inference and highlights key challenges that arise from sparse and heterogeneous data. After that, Section
3.3
briefly describes the proposed Bayesian learning framework.

3.1
SDE-based pKG Mechanistic Model of SRN

For a multi-scale bioprocess mechanistic model with a modular design characterizing the causal interdependency from the molecular to macroscopic scale, the state transition dynamics and inherent stochasticity are induced by random intermolecular interactions (such as collisions, binds, and enzymatic reactions). It accounts for molecular structure-function dynamics by using
Langevin dynamics
, which balances deterministic regulation from the potential energy gradient with stochastic fluctuations due to thermal Brownian motion
(
Zheng et al. 2023a
,
Zheng et al. 2024
,
Wang et al. 2024c
)
. Specifically, at any time
t
, let
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=(s_{t}^{1},s_{t}^{2},\ldots,s_{t}^{N_{s}})^{\top}
represent the state of the bioprocess, where
s_{t}^{j}
denotes the concentration of species
j
for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{s}
and
N_{s}
is the number of species in the system. These species may include environmental factors such as pH, defined by the concentration of free hydrogen ions, i.e.,
\mbox{pH}=-\log([\mbox{H}^{+}])
, as well as metabolites and ions that influence the structure-function relationships and interactions of biomolecules (e.g., proteins, DNA, and RNA).

(a) Discrete-time Formulation of SRN.
As this paper considers Bayesian sequential learning of a multi-scale mechanistic foundational model constructed from SRN modules, we begin by reviewing a general SRN composed of
N_{s}
species, denoted by
\boldsymbol{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{N_{s}})^{\top}
, interacting with each other through
N_{r}
reactions. For each
k
-th reaction given by

p_{k1}X_{1}+p_{k2}X_{2}+\cdots+p_{kN_{s}}X_{N_{s}}\xrightarrow{v_{k}}q_{k1}X_{1}+q_{k2}X_{2}+\cdots+q_{kN_{s}}X_{N_{s}},

we assume that the reaction vector
\boldsymbol{C}_{k}=(q_{k1}-p_{k1},q_{k2}-p_{k2},\ldots,q_{kN_{s}}-p_{kN_{s}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N_{s}}
is known for each
k
-th reaction. This vector encodes the net change in molecular counts of the
N_{s}
species resulting from a single occurrence of the
k
-th reaction. Collectively, these vectors form the
stoichiometry matrix
, defined as
\boldsymbol{C}=\left(\boldsymbol{C}_{1},\boldsymbol{C}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{C}_{N_{r}}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{N_{s}\times N_{r}}
, which characterizes the structural relationships between all reactions in the SRN. The entry
C_{jk}
represents the net change in the count of species
X_{j}
due to the
k
-th reaction; a negative value indicates consumption, while a positive value indicates production. And
v_{k}
denotes the reaction rate associated with the
k
-th reaction.

At any time
t
, bioprocess regulatory mechanisms are characterized by reaction rates that depend on the system state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
. These rates are represented by the vector-valued function
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(v_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}),v_{2}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}),\ldots,v_{N_{r}}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{N_{r}}))^{\top}
, specified by unknown mechanistic parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
that define the scope of our Bayesian learning framework. Drawing on domain knowledge, the kinetic models and reaction regulatory structures encode the logical representation of the bioprocessing mechanisms and molecular interactions; refer to
Kyriakopoulos et al. 2018
for further details.

Example 3.1

(Enzyme Kinetics)

To facilitate a clearer understanding of SRN and the scientific interpretation of the mechanistic parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we consider a simple representative example. This example illustrates a general enzymatic reaction involving four biochemical species, i.e., Enzyme, Substrate, Complex, and Product. The catalytic conversion of the substrate into the product via enzymatic action is described by the following sequence of chemical reactions,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Reaction }1:\ {\rm Enzyme}+{\rm Substrate}\xrightarrow{v_{1}}{\rm Complex},\\
\text{Reaction }2:\ {\rm Complex}\xrightarrow{v_{2}}{\rm Enzyme}+{\rm Substrate},\\
\text{Reaction }3:\ {\rm Complex}\xrightarrow{v_{3}}{\rm Enzyme}+{\rm Product},\end{array}

\displaystyle\mbox{ with stoichiometry matrix }\boldsymbol{C}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&1&1\\
-1&1&0\\
1&-1&-1\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

The system state is defined as
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=(s_{t}^{1},s_{t}^{2},s_{t}^{3},s_{t}^{4})^{\top}
representing the molecular concentrations of Enzyme, Substrate, Complex, and Product at time
t
. We consider two common formulations for modeling enzymatic reaction rates: (i) Mass-action kinetics
(
Rao and Arkin 2003
)
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(k_{F}s_{t}^{1}s_{t}^{2},k_{R}s_{t}^{3},k_{cat}s_{t}^{3})^{\top}
; and (ii) Michaelis-Menten (MM) kinetics, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(V_{\max,1}\frac{s_{t}^{1}}{K_{m,1}+s_{t}^{1}}\frac{s_{t}^{2}}{K_{m,2}+s_{t}^{2}},V_{\max,2}\frac{s_{t}^{3}}{K_{m,3}+s_{t}^{3}},V_{\max,3}\frac{s_{t}^{3}}{K_{m,3}+s_{t}^{3}})^{\top}
. In the mass-action model,
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(k_{F},k_{R},k_{cat})
captures the binding, unbinding, and catalytic rates. In the MM model,
\boldsymbol{\theta}
includes the maximal reaction rates
V_{\max,k}
for
k=1,2,3
and the half-saturation constants
K_{m,j}
for species
j=1,2,3,4
.

The dynamics of the SRN are highly nonlinear and nonstationary. To establish theoretical coherence, we begin by examining the transitions in molecular counts. Molecular reactions occur when one molecule collides, binds, and reacts with another while molecules move around randomly driven by stochastic thermodynamics of Brownian motion. Each reaction alters the molecular counts of species by discrete integer amounts. Consequently, the evolution of molecular counts is naturally modeled as a continuous-time Markov jump process
(
Anderson and Kurtz 2011
)
.

Specifically, let
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=(x_{t}^{1},x_{t}^{2},\ldots,x_{t}^{N_{s}})^{\top}
where
x_{t}^{j}
denotes the molecular count of species
j
for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{s}
. And the propensity function associated with the molecular counts, denoted by
\omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{r}
, describes the probability with which the
k
-th reaction occurs per time unit. Then, letting
t_{h}
be the
h
-th time point for
h=1,2,\ldots,H+1
, the transition of molecular counts can be modeled as
\boldsymbol{x}_{t_{h+1}}=\boldsymbol{x}_{t_{h}}+\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}
, where
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}\in\mathbb{N}^{N_{r}}
is a vector that represents the number of occurrences of
N_{r}
molecular reactions in any given time interval
(t_{h},t_{h+1}]
, following a multivariate counting process with parameters depending on propensity functions
\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Thus,
\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}
represents the net amount of reaction outputs that occur during the time interval
(t_{h},t_{h+1}]
. In many studies (see, for example,
Anderson and Kurtz 2011
,
Schnoerr et al. 2016
), one can represent
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}
with the multivariate Poisson process. More precisely, each component of
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}
is modeled as a nonhomogeneous Poisson process, and
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}\sim\mbox{Poisson}(\int_{t_{h}}^{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})\,dt)
.

To characterize the scale of the system, we define a parameter
\Omega
, which can, for instance, correspond to the bioreactor volume within the SRN framework. Under this assumption, the molecular concentration of species is defined as the molecular count divided by the system size, i.e.,
s_{t}^{j}=x_{t}^{j}/\Omega
for each species
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{s}
. And the relation between propensity functions and reaction rates can be written as
\omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})=\Omega v_{k}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)=\Omega v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{r}
. This yields a discrete-time formulation of the state transition model in terms of molecular concentrations, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}=\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}+\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}~~~\mbox{with}~~~\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}\sim\mbox{Poisson}\left(\int_{t_{h}}^{t_{h+1}}\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})\,dt\right).

(1)

Equation (
1
) also reflects the
doubly stochastic
nature of SRN. Specifically, the state transition is governed by a doubly stochastic Poisson process, denoted by
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}
, whose intensity function depends on the current state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
for
t\in(t_{h},t_{h+1}]
. Importantly,
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
itself is a random vector that evolves stochastically over the same time interval. An exact simulation algorithm for this state transition model was originally developed in the context of chemical kinetics by
Gillespie 1977
, using standard discrete event simulation techniques known as the Gillespie algorithm.

Remark 3.2

The proposed framework can be applicable to hybrid models (mechanistic + statistical) that enables us to leverage existing domain knowledge of biomanufacturing process mechanisms and facilitate learning from data. In particular, except for the uncertainty of the mechanistic parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we can incorporate the uncertainty of the model structure. For example, given any time interval
(t_{h},t_{h+1}]
, the state transition can be modeled as
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}=\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}+\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t_{h}}+\boldsymbol{e}_{t_{h}}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}})
with the residual
\boldsymbol{e}_{t_{h}}
introduced to account for the error induced by the mechanistic model structure and the missing critical input factors. By applying the central limit theorem (CLT), the residual follows a Gaussian distribution with mean and variance depending on the current state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
.

(b) Continuous-time Formulation of SRN.
The mechanistic bioprocess model, formulated as a system of continuous-time SDEs, has a modular design that shows a strong potential to integrate heterogeneous multi-scale data collected across diverse biomanufacturing processes
(
Zheng et al. 2024
)
. To characterize the stochastic evolution of the state trajectory
\{\boldsymbol{s}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
, we model it as the solution to a set of coupled SDEs
d\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\mathbb{E}(d\boldsymbol{s}_{t})+\left\{{\rm Cov}(d\boldsymbol{s}_{t})\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}
, where
d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}
is the increment of an
N_{s}
-dimensional standard Brownian motion. This formulation is derived by a diffusion approximation to the underlying Markov jump process following
Gillespie 2000
, which neglects the discrete nature of the reaction count vector
\Delta\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
. Let
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
represent the number of occurrences of
N_{r}
molecular reactions within an infinitesimal time interval
(t,t+dt]
. Assuming that reaction rates remain approximately constant over such intervals,
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
is modeled as a multivariate Poisson process with rate
\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Conditioned on the current state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
, we assume that no two reactions occur simultaneously, implying independence among the components of
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
. Consequently, the expectation and covariance of
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
are given by
\mathbb{E}(d\boldsymbol{R}_{t})=\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})dt
and Cov
(d\boldsymbol{R}_{t})={\rm diag}\{\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})dt\}
, where the
k
-th diagonal entry is
\Omega v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})dt
. Using Itô calculus
(
Allen 2007
)
, we approximate
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
by the SDE
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}=\mathbb{E}(d\boldsymbol{R}_{t})+\left\{{\rm Cov}(d\boldsymbol{R}_{t})\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}=\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})dt+\left\{{\rm diag}\{\Omega\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}
, where
d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}
now denotes an
N_{r}
-dimensional standard Brownian motion. Given the stoichiometry matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
that encodes the structure of the SRN, the change in state is expressed as
d\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{C}d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
. Substituting the SDE for
d\boldsymbol{R}_{t}
yields the continuous-time state transition model

d\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})dt+\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\boldsymbol{C}{\rm diag}\{\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}.

(2)

We refer to Equation (
2
) as
SDE-based pKG mechanistic model of SRN
.

3.2
Likelihood-based Bayesian Inference

The continuous-time SDEs, characterizing the dynamics and inherent stochasticity of a multi-scale bioprocessing state trajectory, enable us to connect heterogeneous data. This paper assumes that the structure of each reaction rate function
v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{r}
is given, and we focus on the inference of unknown mechanistic parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{N_{r}})^{\top}
. To derive a closed-form likelihood, given any time interval
(t_{h},t_{h+1}]
associated with time frames of data collection, we first present a tractable approximation on the state transition density
p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})
of the SDE-based pKG mechanistic model when the time interval is short (we call it dense data collection), and then consider situations with sparse and heterogeneous data with measurement error.

(a) State Transition Density
p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})
of SDE-based Mechanistic Model.
Since we can only observe discrete samples of an underlying continuous-time stochastic process, time discretization methods are employed to approximate Equation (
2
). Several discretization strategies exist; one commonly used approach is the Euler-Maruyama (EM) approximation
(
Kloeden and Platen 1992
)
. Let the time interval between two consecutive observations be denoted by
\Delta t_{h}=t_{h+1}-t_{h}>0
for
h=1,2,\ldots,H
. Under the EM scheme, the state transition density from
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
to
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}
is given by

\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}+\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})\Delta t_{h},\ \Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{C}{\rm diag}\{\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\Delta t_{h}\right).

(3)

Importantly,
\Delta t_{h}
may vary within or across trajectories, allowing likelihood-based Bayesian inference to accommodate multi-scale data collected from diverse biomanufacturing processes.

Example 3.3

(Lotka-Volterra Model)

Suppose that we have observations
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
at evenly spaced times
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{H+1}
with the length of time interval
\Delta t
. We use a simple Lotka-Volterra model (see
16.1
for a detailed discussion) to illustrate the approximation errors between the exact samples simulated from the model (
1
) by the Gillespie algorithm and the diffusion approximation samples simulated from the model (
3
) with different values of
\Delta t
. In Figure
3.3
, we find that the approximation error vanishes as
\Delta t
gets smaller.

\FIGURE

(a)

\Delta t=2
.

(b)

\Delta t=1
.

(c)

\Delta t=0.5
.

(d)

\Delta t=0.1
.

Simulated observations of prey using Gillespie algorithm and diffusion approximation with different values of
\Delta t
.
One exact sample trajectory and mean value of 100 approximate sample trajectories.

(b) Heterogeneous and Sparse Observations with Measurement Error.
Considering the time and cost of different assay technologies, we often have sparse and heterogeneous observations on partially observed state with random measurement errors. Given the observed dataset denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}=\{\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)}\}_{h=1}^{H+1}\}_{i=1}^{M}
, where
M
is the batch number of experimental run, and
H+1
is the number of observations (or measurements) collected during an experimental run. For notational simplicity, suppose that the same
H
applies to all experimental runs, although it can vary between different experimental runs. The observations at
t_{h}
can be modeled in the form of a linear additive Gaussian,

\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}=\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t_{h}},

(4)

where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t_{h}}
denotes the measurement errors following a multivariate Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}})
. Here,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}}
is a diagonal matrix with
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t_{h}}=(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}}
, the vector of measurement error levels at each observation time
t_{h}
, on the main diagonal, where
\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}\subset[N_{s}]
is the set of observed component indices of the state at
t_{h}
. And
\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}
is a
|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}|
-by-
N_{s}
constant matrix mapping the whole
N_{s}
-dimensional underlying state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
to the
|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}|
-dimensional vector containing only the counterpart of observed components at
t_{h}
, where
|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}|\leq N_{s}
is the cardinality of
\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}
representing the dimension of observed components of
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
. Note that
|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}|
can change at different observation times accounting for the fact that the measures of the partially observed state are asynchronous. Furthermore, let
\boldsymbol{J}_{y}=\cup_{h=1}^{H+1}\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h}}
be the index set of components that can be observed at certain times of the experimental run, and thus
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\boldsymbol{J}_{y}}
represents the vector of measurement error levels of all observed components.

(c) Bayesian Inference.
To quantify the model uncertainty, we write the posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
denotes the feasible parameter space, by Bayes’ rule, i.e.,
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\propto p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)p\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, where the prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
can be selected to incorporate the existing knowledge about the parameters. Through the state transition model (
3
) in situations with dense data collection, or the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel proposed in Section
4.3
in situations with sparse data collection, we can derive the likelihood
p(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta})
in closed form and then obtain the explicit form of the posterior. And with the collection of new experiment data denoted by
\Delta\mathcal{D}
, the model uncertainty can be updated by applying Bayes’ rule, i.e.,
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\cup\Delta\mathcal{D})\propto p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})\cdot p(\Delta\mathcal{D}|\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

Furthermore, as it is prohibitive to find a conjugate prior for such a complicated likelihood, we defer to the sampling methods to generate the posterior samples. To illustrate the limitation of classical MCMC approaches, we take one of the most popular MCMC algorithms, i.e., Metropolis random walk (MRW), as an example. The posterior search evolution of the Markov chain in MRW-based algorithms could be
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau+1):=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}^{\prime}(\tau)
, where
\tau
is the index of the iteration step and
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}^{\prime}(\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
is a Gaussian random vector with mean zero and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\prime}
. Thus, the proposal distribution, denoted by
q_{\rm trans}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau+1)|\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)\right)
, is Gaussian distributed with mean
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)
and covariance
2\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\prime}
; that means
q_{\rm trans}
is assumed to be symmetric centered on the current sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)
, making points closer to
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)
more likely to be visited in the next step. And if the variance of the proposal is too low, the acceptance rate will be high, but the mixing is poor since only small steps are ever taken; if the variance of the proposal is too high, it will almost always make proposals rejected, resulting in many wasted likelihood evaluations before a successful move step. Therefore, due to an inappropriate selection of the proposal distribution, this kind of method can suffer from slow exploration of the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, especially when the parameter dimension is large.

Another widely used class of MCMC algorithms is based on Gibbs sampling. In
10
, we derive a nested Gibbs sampling procedure using the state transition density in Equation (
3
). However, this approach proves to be computationally inefficient due to two key challenges, i.e., the double stochasticity inherent in SRNs and the highly nonlinear nature of the reaction rate functions.

3.3
Description of Proposed Bayesian Learning Framework

In the following sections, we focus on scenarios involving sparse data collection and introduce a mechanism-informed Bayesian learning framework for SRNs, termed RAPTOR-GEN. The overall architecture is illustrated in Figure
3.3
, and the framework comprises two key components:
(i) Bayesian Updating pKG-LNA Metamodel (Section
4
).
We construct an interpretable metamodel by applying the LNA to the pKG mechanistic model formulated in SDE form. This surrogate model approximates the complex state transition densities and is coupled with a sequential learning strategy on the pKG to integrate sparse and noisy measurements from diverse biomanufacturing processes. The metamodel enables inference of heterogeneous latent state variables, thereby facilitating explicit approximation of the otherwise intractable likelihood function.
(ii) LD-LNA Posterior Sampling Method (Section
5
).
We develop an accelerated Bayesian posterior sampling algorithm, LD-LNA, which leverages LD to speed up the search of the target posterior sampling process. By utilizing the gradient of the derived likelihood function, LD-LNA efficiently explores the parameter space and generates posterior samples of the inferred parameters. Furthermore, by generalizing the LNA framework, LD-LNA circumvents the challenge of step size selection. We evaluate both the finite-sample and asymptotic performance of LD-LNA, demonstrating its fast and provable convergence.

To facilitate practical implementation of RAPTOR-GEN, in Section
6
, we design a one-stage iterative algorithm inspired by the LD-LNA derivation, and further improve its computational efficiency by reconstructing it into a two-stage iterative algorithm, enabling rapid and robust generation of posterior samples for mechanistic parameters. We provide a theoretical analysis of the controllable error bounds and prove the convergence of the proposed iterative schemes.

\FIGURE

An illustration of the proposed Bayesian learning framework.
Blue box for the mechanistic model and data, yellow box for the key methodologies adopted, pink box for the key intermediates, green box for two key ingredients of Bayesian learning framework and its implementation algorithm.

Remark 3.4

The proposed Bayesian learning framework can also incorporate the effect of decision making. For biomanufacturing processes, the impact of decisions
\boldsymbol{a}_{t_{h}}
(e.g., feeding strategy and pH control by adding base) on the state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
is often known and happens immediately; that means we get the post-decision state denoted by
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}},\boldsymbol{a}_{t_{h}})
with a known function
\boldsymbol{f}
; for example, in cell culture, we consider the decision
{a}_{t_{h}}
as the exchange of media and its impact on the concentration of the solution in the bioreactor leads to the post-decision state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}\cdot(1-a_{t_{h}})+\boldsymbol{s}_{in}\cdot a_{t_{h}}
, where
a_{t_{h}}
is the percentage of media exchanged and
\boldsymbol{s}_{in}
represents the concentration of the added fresh media (suppose it is given). Thus, the proposed Bayesian learning framework can be easily extended to situations accounting for decision impact and guide biomanufacturing process optimal control through the proposed RL scheme on the policy-augmented Bayesian pKG model
(
Zheng et al. 2023b
)
accounting for model uncertainty and facilitating online data fusion and knowledge update.

4
Linear Noise Approximation (LNA) on SDEs with Enhancement

In this paper, the LNA concept is adopted to two types of SDEs, one is the pKG model in SDE form, and the other is the LD for the posterior sampling process. Thus, we present LNA as an approximation for general SDEs in Section
4.1
. Section
4.2
applies LNA to the SDE-based pKG model, providing a tractable state transition density of the system that enables us to infer latent state variables and derive the likelihood in closed form. To mitigate the approximation error inherent in the naive LNA, which tends to accumulate as the state transition time interval and the time variable
t
in the SDEs increase, Section
4.3
introduces an enhanced approach. Specifically, we refine the naive LNA by applying Bayesian updates to the inferred state variables at each observation time point, incorporating partially observed and noisy measurements. This enhancement improves inference accuracy and robustness under sparse data collection conditions.

4.1
LNA for General SDEs

We formulate the LNA for a general SDE model in favor of an informal derivation to introduce its heuristic idea, and refer the readers to
Ferm et al. 2008
for a more rigorous derivation and a more detailed discussion. Consider a general SDE satisfied by a process
\{\boldsymbol{z}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
of the form

d\boldsymbol{z}_{t}=\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})dt+\epsilon\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})d\boldsymbol{B}_{t},

(5)

where
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})
and
\epsilon\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})
are the drift and diffusion terms, respectively, and
\epsilon
is used to indicate the size of the stochastic perturbation. The idea of LNA is to assume that the path
\{\boldsymbol{z}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
of the SDE (
5
) can be approximated by a path
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
, which can be partitioned into a deterministic path
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
and a stochastic perturbation
\{\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
from this path, represented by

\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}.

(6)

Under this assumption, by replacing
\boldsymbol{z}_{t}
in SDE (
5
) with (
6
), we have
d\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\right)=\boldsymbol{a}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\right)dt+\epsilon\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\right)d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}
. Through a Taylor expansion of the SDE around
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}
up to order
\epsilon
, we obtain

d\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}+\epsilon d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}=\boldsymbol{a}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)dt+\epsilon\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{z}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}dt+\epsilon\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}.

(7)

Since the deterministic path means that
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}
is a constant given any fixed time
t
, while stochastic perturbation means that
\boldsymbol{M}_{t}
is a random variable given any fixed time
t
, following the idea of ODE splitting operation as an approximation to facilitate the solving of ODEs, we split Equation (
7
) into one deterministic ODE in (
8
) and one SDE in (
9
). Specifically, let
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}
be the solution to the ODE

d\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}=\boldsymbol{a}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)dt

(8)

with initial value
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{0}
, and collecting the remaining terms of Equation (
7
) gives the SDE

d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{z}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}dt+\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)d\boldsymbol{B}_{t}.

(9)

For any Gaussian distributed initial condition on
\boldsymbol{M}_{0}
, the increment in (
9
) is a linear combination of Gaussians. Thus, the solution to SDE (
9
) follows a Gaussian distribution for all time
t\geq 0
, denoted by
\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{m}_{t},\boldsymbol{K}_{t}\right)
, where the mean vector
\boldsymbol{m}_{t}
and the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{K}_{t}
can be obtained by utilizing Proposition
4.1
. The proof is given in
13.1
.

Proposition 4.1

The mean vector
\boldsymbol{m}_{t}
and the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{K}_{t}
of the Gaussian variable
\boldsymbol{M}_{t}
for any
t>0
can be obtained by solving the ODEs in (
10
) and (
11
) with initial values
\boldsymbol{m}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{K}_{0}
,

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{m}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{z}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}dt,

(10)

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{K}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{t}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{z}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{z}=\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{K}_{t}+\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)dt.

(11)

For the initial values, let
\boldsymbol{m}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{0}
, then
\boldsymbol{m}_{t}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
t\geq 0
according to the ODE (
10
). Suppose that the initial condition for the SDE (
5
) is
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{*},\epsilon^{2}\boldsymbol{Z}_{0}^{*})
, then we set
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{K}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{Z}_{0}^{*}
. By doing this, we reduce the number of ODEs from three to two: integrating (
8
) and (
11
) through 0 to time
t
provides the final result
\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t},\epsilon^{2}\boldsymbol{K}_{t}\right)
, from which we find that the LNA gives us a Gaussian approximation to the state transition density of the system represented by SDEs.

4.2
LNA for pKG of SRN

For the SDE-based pKG in (
2
) with an emphasis on nonlinear
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
characterizing the regulatory mechanisms of SRN, its nonlinear drift and diffusion terms make solving it directly to obtain the metamodel of
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
requires time-consuming numerical integration methods. By applying the LNA to the SDE (
2
), we obtain a computationally efficient metamodel
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}
that surrogates the true state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
. Specifically, we have
\boldsymbol{z}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
,
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t}):=\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{C}{\rm diag}\{\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\epsilon:=\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}
, and
\boldsymbol{K}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
can be approximated by a multivariate Gaussian distribution
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}
with mean vector
\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}
and covariance matrix
\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}
obtained by solving the ODEs (
8
) and (
11
).

Further combined with the assumption of a linear additive Gaussian relation between each observation
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}
and the underlying state value
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
in (
4
), a tractable approximation of the likelihood of observations
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
can be derived. Compared to other black-box metamodels such as GPs, the LNA for the pKG is derived directly from the SDE (
2
), which fully exploits the structural information of the state transition provided by such an SDE, thus improving the interpretability. For completeness, we provide the results of the asymptotic convergence of LNA for pKG in
11.1
.

Remark 4.2

In general SDEs, the scaling parameter
\epsilon
in Equations (
5
) and (
6
) adjusts the magnitude of the diffusion term
\epsilon\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})
and stochastic perturbation
\epsilon\boldsymbol{M}_{t}
relative to the drift term
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})
and the deterministic path
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t}
, and facilitates the grouping of terms after Taylor expansion. When applying LNA to Equation (
2
), we set
\epsilon:=\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, reflecting the intuition that fluctuations in molecular concentrations
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
diminish as the bioreactor volume
\Omega
increases. This scaling enables the derivation of an explicit convergence bound for LNA toward the stationary distribution of a properly scaled SRN (see Section
11.1
and
Grunberg and Del Vecchio 2023
). The empirical results in Section
7
further demonstrate LNA’s robustness even at unit system size (
\Omega:=1
).

4.3
Enhance LNA Metamodel through Bayesian Updating on the pKG

With the LNA applied to the pKG in the SDE form as discussed in Section
4.1
, the ODEs (
8
) and (
11
) are solved once throughout the time interval for given initial values of state, which could lead to a poor LNA metamodel since the approximation error accumulates as
t
grows. To address this issue, we enhance the LNA metamodel through sequential Bayesian updating on the pKG and develop an interpretable Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel on state transition dynamics.

The enhancement strategy involves sequentially updating the LNA metamodel
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}
at each observation time
t_{h}
using all prior observations up to that point (see Figure
4.3
). Specifically, we combine the LNA-based latent state model
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h-1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}})
with the conditional prediction distribution of the observation
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h-1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}})
derived via Equation (
4
), yielding the joint distribution

\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}\\
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}\end{pmatrix}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h-1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}\\
\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}&\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}\\
\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}&\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Using the standard properties of multivariate Gaussian distributions, the posterior distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}
given
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}
is
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t_{h}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t_{h}})
, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t_{h}}

\displaystyle:=\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}+\Omega\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}}\right)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}-\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}\right),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t_{h}}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}^{\top}+\Omega\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}.

This enables us to probe the latent state based on the partial observations with measurement error.

\FIGURE

An illustration of Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel.

Then, the values of the ODEs (
8
) and (
11
) at time
t_{h}
(i.e.,
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t_{h}}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h}}
and
\boldsymbol{K}_{t_{h}}:=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h}}
) are reset to
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t_{h}}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t_{h}}
, respectively. By integrating these two updated ODEs over time
t_{h}
to
t_{h+1}
, we obtain
\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}
, also the LNA metamodel
\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}})
. Combined with the linear additive Gaussian measurement error in (
4
), the prediction of the observation
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}
is tractable in the form of a conditional distribution
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}})
. Finally, the likelihood approximation for the historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
can be constructed by decomposition,
p(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\prod_{i=1}^{M}\{p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\prod_{h=1}^{H}p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\}
. We refer the readers to
11.2
and the preliminary work
(
Xu and Xie 2024
)
for more details.

Therefore, the pKG mechanistic model is formulated as a continuous-time SDE system that captures the dynamics and intrinsic stochasticity of multi-scale bioprocess trajectories. This formulation enables the integration of heterogeneous data from diverse sources and manufacturing systems. To address the challenge of fusing such complex datasets with distributed latent state variables, we propose a Bayesian sequential updating pKG-LNA metamodel. This metamodel facilitates closed-form likelihood derivation for
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
and accelerates Bayesian learning on the pKG.

5
Langevin Diffusion-Based Linear Noise Approximation (LD-LNA)

This section introduces LD to speed up the search for the target Bayesian posterior sampling process in Section
5.1
and then extends the LNA framework to LD, resulting in the proposed LD-LNA Bayesian inference method (Section
5.2
). We analyze its finite-sample and asymptotic performance in Section
5.3
. For notational simplicity, we denote the inferred parameters by an
N_{\theta}
-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, which includes both unknown mechanistic parameters and measurement error levels.

5.1
Langevin Diffusion (LD) to Accelerate Posterior Sampling

To overcome the limitations of random walk-based strategies in classical MCMC (see Section
3.2
), we adopt LD, which leverages the gradient of the posterior
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
. This gradient captures the interdependencies of the parameters that explain the heterogeneous and sparse observations
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
from various biomanufacturing processes. LD constructs a continuous-time stochastic process governed by the following SDE,

d\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)},d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau),

(12)

where
d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
denotes a standard Brownian motion of
N_{\theta}
-dimensional. This formulation improves MCMC mixing efficiency by guiding the sampling process toward regions of high posterior density, with the gradient acting as a drift term analogous to the gradient flow. To support this approach, we introduce Assumption
5.1
on the log-posterior, and Theorem
5.1
establishes that the LD process admits the target posterior as its unique stationary distribution. The proof is provided in
13.2
.

{assumption}

The log-posterior distribution
\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is
L
-smooth. That is, it is continuously differentiable and its gradient
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is Lipschitz continuous with the Lipschitz constant
L
.

Theorem 5.1

Under Assumption
5.1
, the probability density function (PDF) of
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)
generated by the LD (
12
) approaches a stationary distribution
q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
in the limit of
\tau
, and
q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
.

To implement the theoretical result in Theorem
5.1
, we employ the Euler-Maruyama approximation on (
12
) to obtain the discretized LD with fixed step size
\Delta\tau=\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
and generate an approximate posterior sampling process, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k}),

(13)

where each
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
is a Gaussian random vector with mean zero and covariance matrix diag
\{\Delta\tau\}
. Thus, the proposal distribution
q_{\rm trans}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\right)
is Gaussian distributed with mean
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\Delta\tau
and covariance diag
\{2\Delta\tau\}
. This LD implementation procedure is also termed
unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA)

(
Roberts and Tweedie 1996
,
Xifara et al. 2014
)
, which utilizes the gradient of log-posterior to facilitate the exploitation over a parameter space with higher posterior probability and quickly generate more promising samples. We summarize the ULA procedure for Bayesian posterior sampling tailored for SRN in
12
.

Remark 5.2

From Equation (
13
), the support of the posterior samples is the whole
N_{\theta}
-dimensional real space
\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
. But in most real-word cases, the feasible parameter space of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is restricted; for example, some biological parameters such as rates should ensure positivity, while some parameters such as probabilities or bioavailability should be between 0 and 1
(
Prague et al. 2013
)
. Reparametrization of the system allows us to take these constraints into account. Specifically, we can introduce one-to-one functions
g_{k}(\cdot)
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, and define transformed parameters
\theta_{k}^{\rm trans}=g_{k}(\theta_{k})
; for example,
g_{k}(\cdot)
can be logarithmic functions to transform the support of parameters from positive space to real space, or inverse logistic functions to transform parameters between 0 and 1 to real space. Then we can perform ULA on the transformed parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\rm trans}=(\theta_{1}^{\rm trans},\theta_{2}^{\rm trans},\ldots,\theta_{N_{\theta}}^{\rm trans})^{\top}
.

5.2
LNA for LD

The discretization of LD in Equation (
12
) with step size
\Delta\tau
introduces bias into the posterior sampling process for
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
. A larger
\Delta\tau
accelerates exploration of the parameter space but increases bias, causing the trajectory
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
generated by the update rule in Equation (
13
) to deviate significantly from the true posterior distribution
(
Durmus and Moulines 2019
)
. A small step size
\Delta\tau
reduces bias in the discretized LD but requires many iterations to adequately explore the parameter space. To address the challenge of step size selection in discretized LD, we adopt the LNA framework and introduce Assumption
5.2
.

{assumption}

Suppose that the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is convex. The path
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
of LD (
12
) can be approximated by a path
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
, which can be partitioned into a deterministic path
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
and a stochastic perturbation
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
as

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau).

(14)

And
\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is three times differentiable in a neighborhood of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
denoted by
N_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}:\ d(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau),\boldsymbol{\theta})<\varepsilon\}
with
\varepsilon
a small positive number and
d(\cdot,\cdot)
a distance measure.

From LD (
12
), we find that the support of the path
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
is the whole
N_{\theta}
-dimensional real space
\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
; this means the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
, which is obviously convex. We can also use the reparameterization method in Remark
5.2
to transform the original parameter space to the real space, and thus it satisfies the convexity of the parameter space in Assumption
5.2
.

Following similar operations as in Section
4.1
with
\epsilon:=1
, we have

d\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+d\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}d\tau+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau),

(15)

for which we split into one ODE and one SDE with respective solutions
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
as

\displaystyle d\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}d\tau,

(16)

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau),

(17)

with initial values
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}(0)
, where
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
is Gaussian for any Gaussian distributed
\boldsymbol{\xi}(0)
, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau),\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)\right)
. Its mean
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)
can be obtained by solving two ODEs as summarized in Proposition
13.3
in
13.3
, which comes directly from Proposition
4.1
.

To take advantage of the limiting property of LD shown in Theorem
5.1
, we first let
\tau\to\infty
in ODE (
16
) and denote its solution in the limit of
\tau
as
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\in{\rm int}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
, which is an equilibrium point of this ODE. Thus,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
can be regarded as a local maximum of
\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
satisfying
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}=0
. Then, let
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
represent the solution of SDE (
17
) with
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau):=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
, that is,

d\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau),

(18)

indicating that
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
is Gaussian for all
\tau\geq 0
once the initial condition
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(0)
is Gaussian, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau),\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)\right)
, whose mean
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)
can be obtained by solving two ODEs in Proposition
13.4
in
13.3
.

Remark 5.3

The SDE (
18
) is equivalent to that obtained by applying the LNA in Section
4.1
to the LD (
12
) under
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
, which means that the deterministic path in Assumption
5.2
is a constant path
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\}
and the stochastic perturbation away from this path is denoted by
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
.

Theorem
5.4
shows the limiting distribution of
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\infty),\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)\right)
, where
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\infty)=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)=(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
are respective equilibrium points of the ODEs about
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)
(i.e., Equations (
34
) and (
35
)). The proof is provided in
13.3
.

Theorem 5.4

Suppose that Assumption
5.2
holds. The limiting distribution of
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
.

Notice that
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\infty)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)
, and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)
. Then,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)
, and the limiting distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
that we call
LD-LNA
can be immediately obtained in Corollary
5.5
, which provides an approximate posterior distribution.

Corollary 5.5

(LD-LNA)

Suppose that Assumption
5.2
holds. The stationary distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
is distributed according to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*},(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1})
.

Remark 5.6

Corollary
5.5
recovers the well-known BvM theorem
(
Le Cam 1986
,
Van der Vaart 2000
)
. The BvM theorem states that, under regularity conditions, the posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
converges in the limit of infinite data to a Gaussian distribution with the posterior mode of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
as mean and the inverse of the observed Fisher information matrix at the posterior mode as covariance. This links Bayesian inference with frequentist large-sample theory. The proposed LD-LNA can be viewed as the BvM theorem derived from the likelihood-based Bayesian posterior sampling perspective that is different from any previous literature
(
Bickel and Kleijn 2012
,
Bochkina 2019
)
. This difference gives the LD-LNA an advantage over the original BvM theorem. As the implementation of the BvM theorem faces the challenge in complicated cases such as sparse data collection and a high dimension of inferred parameters, it is computationally inefficient to compute the posterior mode and the inverse of the observed Fisher information matrix. The derivation of the LD-LNA inspires us to design an efficient solution scheme to fast and robustly implement this theoretical result and generate posterior samples; see the algorithm development in Section
6
.

Remark 5.7

Corollary
5.5
also indicates that the proposed LD-LNA Bayesian inference approach reconciles Bayesian posterior sampling and Bayesian exact computation. For Bayesian inference in biomanufacturing, since a conjugate prior is hard to find for such a complicated likelihood, the exact Bayesian posterior rarely belongs to any standard distribution family, making the Bayesian exact computation method analytically intractable. The LD-LNA is derived from LD, one of the Bayesian posterior sampling methods, and analytically converges to a standard distribution as does the Bayesian exact computation method. Compared to typical ABC-type and MCMC-type Bayesian posterior sampling methods that dominate the literature and require significant computational cost to generate each posterior sample, LD-LNA inherits the advantages of Bayesian exact computation, that is, it can generate sufficient posterior samples from the converged standard distribution at once with negligible computational cost; see Section
7
for empirical performance.

Now we analyze the posterior approximation performance of LD-LNA in Corollary
5.5
. For clarity, we write down the Taylor expansion of the drift term of LD (
12
) around
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
under Assumption
5.2
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)),

(19)

where
\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))=\left(\mathcal{O}(||\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)||)\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
is the remainder with
\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}
denoting an
N_{\theta}
-by-
N_{\theta}
matrix of ones. Theorem
5.8
states that the approximation error of LD-LNA caused by the remainder of the Taylor expansion can be ignored under the condition tr
(\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty))\to 0
, where tr
(\cdot)
is the trace of a matrix. This condition implies that
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]\to 0
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
(see the proof of Theorem
5.8
in
13.4
), where
R_{k}(\cdot)
is the
k
-th component of the Lagrange form of the remainder vector
\boldsymbol{R}(\cdot)
(see
13.4
for its specific form). This means that the approximation error between the path
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
of LD (
12
) and the approximate path
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
in the limit of
\tau
is negligible when the mean of the remainder in the Taylor expansion (
19
) in the limit of
\tau
is 0, then the stationary distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
converges to the same as that generated by ordinary LD (
12
).

Theorem 5.8

Suppose that Assumption
5.2
holds. If tr
(\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty))\to 0
, then the stationary distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
converges to the target posterior distribution
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
as that generated by LD (
12
).

5.3
Finite-Sample Convergence Analysis of LD-LNA

Analogous to how fluctuations in molecular concentrations diminish as the bioreactor volume increases, fluctuations in parameter estimates tend to vanish as the data size grows.
