However, unlike the explicit scaling between concentration and volume, the relationship between parameter uncertainty and data size is implicit in the posterior distribution, making it difficult to derive a quantitative bound for assessing finite-sample performance of the LD-LNA approximation. In this section, we establish an explicit bound as a function of data size, which we then use to characterize the asymptotic behavior as the data size approaches infinity.

For notational simplicity, we denote the historical observations up to time
t_{h}
in the
i
-th trajectory as
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)}=(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})
. Following the likelihood decomposition
p(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta})=\prod_{i=1}^{M}\{p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\prod_{h=1}^{H}p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}
from Section
4.3
, each term is Gaussian distributed under the pKG-LNA framework. For generality, we analyze convergence under the assumption that the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
(for
h=1,2,\ldots,H
and
i=1,2,\ldots,M
) belong to the exponential family.

We express the conditional distributions in canonical exponential family form for analytical convenience. Specifically,
p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta})=p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))=\exp\{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})-g(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})-A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\}
and
p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})=p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))=\exp\{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-g(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))\}
, where
K_{h}
for
h=1,\ldots,H+1
are respective numbers of natural parameters for each conditional distribution. The vectors
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=(\eta_{1}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\ldots,\eta_{K_{1}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(\eta_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}),\ldots,\eta_{K_{h+1}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}
are functions of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)}
, respectively. The functions
g(\cdot)
,
A(\cdot)
, and
T_{k}(\cdot)
are known and specific to the distribution family. We adopt the canonical form for its analytical tractability, noting that any exponential family distribution can be reparameterized accordingly. See Example
13.10
in
13.5
for the reparameterization of the multivariate Gaussian conditional distribution used in Section
4.3
. Applying Bayes’ rule, the posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
given the historical dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
is
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})=\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})\exp\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})]-\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}
, where the normalization constant is
\rho(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\exp\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})]-\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})\}\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{M}[A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\sum_{h=1}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))]-\log(p(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
; see
13.5
for the derivation.

To evaluate the accuracy of the LD-LNA posterior approximation, we measure its proximity to the true posterior distribution
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
using the 1-Wasserstein distance. Specifically, we compare a standard Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{Z}
with the standardized posterior

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)=\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right),\text{ where }\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right),

(20)

where
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
is the posterior mode. The 1-Wasserstein distance, widely used in Bayesian analysis
(
Gibbs and Su 2002
)
, is defined in Definition
5.9
.

Definition 5.9

(1-Wasserstein Distance)

Let
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be random vectors with distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, respectively. The 1-Wasserstein distance is
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\right)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{W}_{1}}|\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{X})]-\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{Y})]|
, where
\mathcal{W}_{1}=\left\{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ \forall\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},|h(\boldsymbol{x})-h(\boldsymbol{y})|\leq||\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}||\right\}
.

We rely on Assumption
5.3
to support the convergence analysis. These conditions are readily verified for the specific forms of the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,M
and
h=1,2,\ldots,H
. The four components of Assumption
5.3
are essential for the proof of Theorem
5.10
, with the derivative bound (c) and moment bound (d) specifically used to control the 1-Wasserstein distance and define the constant
C
in the theorem.

{assumption}

Suppose that the following claims hold.

(a)

The posterior distribution
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\to 0
when
\boldsymbol{\theta}
approaches the boundary of its support.

(b)

\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,K_{1}
, and
\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,K_{h+1}
,
h=1,2,\ldots,H
,
i=1,2,\ldots,M
, and
\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})
have third-order partial derivatives in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

(c)

(Derivative Bound). There exists a constant
M_{1}>0
such that for any
1\leq j,l,n\leq N_{\theta}
, we have
\left|\frac{\partial^{3}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{j}\partial\theta_{l}\partial\theta_{n}}\right|\leq M_{1}
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

(d)

(Moment Bound). There exists a constant
M_{2}>0
such that for any
1\leq l,n\leq N_{\theta}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
follows a unique stationary distribution
q
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\sim q}\left|\tilde{\theta}_{l}(\infty)\tilde{\theta}_{n}(\infty)\right|\leq M_{2}
.

Theorem
5.10
establishes an upper bound on the 1-Wasserstein distance between the standardized posterior distribution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
and a standard Gaussian vector
\boldsymbol{Z}
. The proof, provided in
13.5
, highlights how the dimensionality of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
influences the discrepancy between these distributions. Notably, the largest eigenvalue of the matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
, denoted by
\lambda_{\max}
, serves as a key indicator of the approximation quality.

Theorem 5.10

Suppose that Assumption
5.3
holds, and the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
(
i=1,\ldots,M
,
h=1,\ldots,H
) belong to the exponential family. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
be the standardized posterior defined in Equation (
20
), and let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an
N_{\theta}
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector. Then, there exists a constant
C=\frac{1}{2}M_{1}M_{2}
such that
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\leq CN_{\theta}^{\frac{9}{2}}\lambda_{\max}^{\frac{3}{2}}
, where
\lambda_{\max}>0
is the largest eigenvalue of the matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
.

Building on Theorem
5.10
, we derive a finite-sample bound in Corollary
5.11
, showing that the 1-Wasserstein distance between the distributions of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
scales as
(M(H+1))^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. In cases of sparse data collection, as described in Equation (
4
) and the Bayesian updating process of the pKG-LNA metamodel, the 1-Wasserstein distance scales as
(M\sum_{h=0}^{H}(|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|^{2}))^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. This expression highlights its sensitivity to the number of observed components at each observation time, reflecting how data sparsity influences the convergence behavior. The proof is provided in
13.6
. The 1-Wasserstein distance offers an intuitive interpretation: it quantifies the “work” required to transform one distribution into another
(
Sommerfeld and Munk 2018
)
. For any fixed data size, we obtain a corresponding distribution for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
. As more real-world data are collected, this distribution evolves through intermediate forms, reflecting the sequential update of posterior beliefs, and ultimately converges toward a standard Gaussian distribution at a rate of
\mathcal{O}((M(H+1))^{-\frac{3}{2}})
.

Corollary 5.11

(Finite-Sample Bound)

Suppose that Assumption
5.3
holds, and the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
(
i=1,\ldots,M
and
h=1,\ldots,H
) belong to the exponential family. Then, for the standardized posterior
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
defined in (
20
), and an
N_{\theta}
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{Z}
, the 1-Wasserstein distance satisfies
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\mathcal{O}((M(H+1))^{-\frac{3}{2}})
. Furthermore, in the case of sparse data collection described in Equation (
4
) and governed by the likelihood derived from the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel, the 1-Wasserstein distance satisfies
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\mathcal{O}((M\sum_{h=0}^{H}(|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|^{2}))^{-\frac{3}{2}})
, revealing how the convergence rate depends on the number and structure of the observed components at each observation time, emphasizing the impact of data sparsity on the posterior approximation quality.

Moreover, based on the above nonasymptotic result in Corollary
5.11
, we obtain the asymptotic convergence of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
to
\boldsymbol{Z}
as the data size goes to infinity (i.e.,
M(H+1)\to\infty
); see Corollary
5.12
. This result confirms that, as the data size grows sufficiently large and the parameter dimension
N_{\theta}
remains appropriately bounded, the LD-LNA posterior approximation converges to the true posterior distribution.

Corollary 5.12

(Asymptotic Convergence)

Suppose that Assumption
5.3
holds, and the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
(
i=1,\ldots,M
and
h=1,\ldots,H
) belong to the exponential family. Then, for the standardized posterior
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
defined in Equation (
20
), and an
N_{\theta}
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{Z}
, we have
\lim_{M(H+1)\to\infty}d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=0
.

From the results of Theorem
5.10
, Corollary
5.11
, and Corollary
5.12
, we conclude that as the size of the historical dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
goes to infinity, the largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\max}
of
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
tends to zero. Since tr
[(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}]=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\lambda_{i}\leq N_{\theta}\lambda_{\max}
, where
\lambda_{i}
are the eigenvalues of the matrix, it follows that the trace also vanishes as the data size increases. This leads to the conclusion in Theorem
5.8
, confirming that its asymptotic behavior is consistent with Corollary
5.12
.

6
RAPTOR-GEN Algorithm Development

The LD-LNA framework provides an approximation to the target posterior sampling process, as stated in Corollary
5.5
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*},(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1})
. However, implementing this result poses practical challenges, particularly under sparse data collection with measurement noise. In such cases, the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel must be used within RAPTOR-GEN to approximate the likelihood (see Section
4.3
). This makes solving
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}=0
to obtain
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
analytically difficult, and computing the inverse Hessian becomes computationally expensive in high-dimensional settings. To address these issues, Section
6.1
introduces an intuitive solution scheme for RAPTOR-GEN, inspired by the LD-LNA derivation in Section
5.2
. This scheme solves a system of ODEs to approximate the limiting distribution of LD in Equation (
12
). Theoretical support for its convergence is provided in Section
6.2
. Furthermore, Section
6.3
improves computational efficiency by reformulating the scheme into a two-stage iterative algorithm, enabling fast and robust posterior sampling for parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

6.1
Solution Scheme for LD-LNA

Following the derivation in Section
5.2
, to obtain two components
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
and
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
in LD-LNA, it is sufficient to solve two ODEs about
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)
(i.e., Equations (
16
) and (
33
)) in the limit of
\tau
. For each ODE, we employ the Euler method that converts a continuous ODE into a system of discrete approximations that can be solved iteratively. Proposition
6.1
summarizes this solution scheme.

Proposition 6.1

Two components
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
and
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
in LD-LNA can be obtained by simultaneously running two iterative processes, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})

\displaystyle:=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\Delta\tau,

(21)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\left[\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\right)^{\top}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right]\Delta\tau,

(22)

from respective initial values
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
for iteration steps
N
so that
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
and
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}:=\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
, where
\Delta\tau=\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
is the fixed step size,
N
is the iteration step until some specified termination criteria are satisfied, and
\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}
is an
N_{\theta}
-by-
N_{\theta}
identity matrix. Then we obtain the approximate LD-LNA denoted by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}))
.

Based on Proposition
6.1
, we develop a one-stage iterative algorithm to implement RAPTOR-GEN in Algorithm
1
. First, we set the prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
used in the explicit calculation of the posterior distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
, and set the initial condition
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
as a sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0),\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)\right)
of LD (
12
). We iteratively solve the coupled equations (
21
) and (
22
) starting from the initial values
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
until convergence is achieved. Specifically, the iteration terminates when both conditions
||\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||\leq\varepsilon_{1}
and
||\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||_{\rm F}\leq\varepsilon_{2}
are satisfied, where
||\cdot||_{\rm F}
denotes the Frobenius norm, and
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
are predefined tolerances. Alternatively, the process may terminate upon reaching a maximum number of iterations
N
. The gradient and Hessian of the log-posterior used in Step 2 are computed based on the explicit likelihood derived from the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel, as discussed in Section
4.3
. Consequently, we generate approximate posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}
by sampling from the Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\right)
, where
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
are the converged solutions from the iterative scheme. If the support of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is constrained (e.g., restricted to the positive orthant), we apply the reparameterization described in Remark
5.2
to map the parameters to real space before executing Algorithm
1
on the transformed variables.

Input:

The prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
, initial condition
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0),\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)\right)
, step size
\Delta\tau
, and tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
(or maximum iteration counts
N
).

Output:

Approximate posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
.

1.
Set the initial values
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)
,
\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}
and
\varepsilon_{\Psi}
to sufficiently large numbers, and
k:=0
;

while

\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}>\varepsilon_{1}
or
\varepsilon_{\Psi}>\varepsilon_{2}

(or
while

k<N
)

do

2.
Calculate
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}
and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}
based on the explicit derivation of likelihood;

3.
Generate
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
through respective Equations (
21
) and (
22
);

4.
Calculate
\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}:=||\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||
and
\varepsilon_{\Psi}:=||\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||_{\rm F}
, and set
k:=k+1
;
5.
Generate posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}
from
\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\right)
based on LD-LNA.

Algorithm 1

One-stage iterative algorithm for implementing RAPTOR-GEN.

6.2
Convergence Analysis of Solution Scheme

This section presents a convergence analysis of the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm (i.e., Algorithm
1
). Specifically, we derive an error bound that quantifies the discrepancy between the output of the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm and the target LD process defined in (
12
). This bound provides theoretical guarantees on the algorithm’s approximation quality and convergence behavior. For notational convenience, we denote the drift term of LD (
12
) as
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
. Then LD (
12
) can be written as
d\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
. Lemma
6.2
indicates that, to analyze the error between the results generated by the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm and LD (
12
), it is sufficient to analyze the error between the results generated by Equations (
23
) and (
12
). The proof is provided in
14.1
, and our analysis builds upon Definition
6.3
, which formalizes the notions of strong and weak convergence for SDEs, as discussed in
Kloeden and Platen 1992
.

Lemma 6.2

The random variable
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
, generated by the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm (i.e., Algorithm
1
), follows the distribution that follows
\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\right)
. This variable is equivalent to the result obtained by iteratively applying the update rule,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1})

(23)

from the initial time
\tau_{0}=0
to the terminal time
\tau_{N}=T
. Here,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}):=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})
with
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right)
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})):=\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
, and
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\text{diag}\{\Delta\tau\}\right)
.

Definition 6.3

(Strong and Weak Convergence)

Let
\{\boldsymbol{x}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
be a stochastic process generated by an SDE. We say that a discrete approximation
\boldsymbol{x}_{\Delta\tau}
over time interval
[0,T]
with fixed step size
\Delta\tau
converges strongly to
\boldsymbol{x}(T)
if
\lim_{\Delta\tau\to 0}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{x}(T)-\boldsymbol{x}_{\Delta\tau}(T)||=0
, and converges weakly to
\boldsymbol{x}(T)
if
\lim_{\Delta\tau\to 0}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\boldsymbol{x}(T)\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\boldsymbol{x}_{\Delta\tau}(T)\right)\right]\right|=0
, where
f
is any continuous differentiable and polynomial growth function.

To ensure the existence and uniqueness of a solution to the Itô process associated with LD in Equation (
12
), we impose three conditions specified in Assumption
6.2
. These conditions guarantee that the following SDE admits a unique solution for any terminal time
T\geq 0
:

\boldsymbol{\theta}(T)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)=\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))\,d\tau+\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{2}\,d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau).

(24)

{assumption}

Suppose that the initialization
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)
is bounded, that is,
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)||^{2}<\infty
(Initialize condition). And let
a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))
be the
i
-th component of
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))
(i.e.,
\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)}
), for
i=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
. We assume that, each
a_{i}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\to\mathbb{R}
where
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
, is a Lipschitz continuous function of linear growth, that is, there exist constants
C_{i,1},C_{i,2}>0
which do not depend on
\Delta\tau
, such that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau),\boldsymbol{\theta}(s)\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
,
\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(s))\right|\leq C_{i,1}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(s)||
(Lipschitz condition), and
\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))\right|^{2}+\left|\sqrt{2}\right|^{2}\leq C_{i,2}^{2}\left(1+||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)||^{2}\right)
(Linear growth condition).

Since the initial distribution is Gaussian with
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)||^{2}<\infty
, which satisfies the initialization condition specified in Assumption
6.2
. For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau),\boldsymbol{\theta}(s)\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, the Lipschitz condition implies
||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(s))||^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(s))\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,1}^{2}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(s)||^{2}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, which shows that
||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(s))||\leq C_{1}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(s)||
with
C_{1}=(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, that is,
\boldsymbol{a}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\to\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
is Lipschitz continuous. It is in line with Assumption
5.1
as
L=C_{1}
. And for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, the linear growth condition implies
||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))||^{2}+||\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}||^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))\right|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|\sqrt{2}\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,2}^{2}\left(1+||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)||^{2}\right)
for
i=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, which shows that
||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))||^{2}+||\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}||^{2}\leq C_{2}^{2}\left(1+||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)||^{2}\right)
with
C_{2}=(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,2}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. That is,
\boldsymbol{a}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\to\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
satisfies a linear growth condition. From the definition of Itô process reviewed in
14.2
, Equation (
24
) has a unique solution.

Recall that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
is the solution to Equation (
15
) with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
; thus, Equation (
15
) can be represented by
d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\left\{\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right\}d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
. For
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, by decomposition:

\displaystyle\quad||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||=||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))+\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))-(\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})))||

\displaystyle\leq||\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))||+||\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))-\boldsymbol{a}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||+||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\underbrace{||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)||}_{\text{LNA error}}+\underbrace{||\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})||}_{\text{discretization error}})+\underbrace{||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||}_{\text{Taylor approximation error}}.

Intuitively, the discrepancy between the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN and the LD drift terms can be attributed to three sources: (i) the LNA approximation error resulting from the approximation of the LD process in Equation (
12
) using the LNA in Equation (
15
); (ii) the discretization error resulting from the discretization of the continuous-time formulation in Equation (
15
), as implemented in its discrete counterpart in Equation (
23
); and (iii) the Taylor approximation error resulting from the remainder of the Taylor expansion in Equation (
23
).

Theorem
6.4
indicates that the strong error of the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm is affected by the remainder of the Taylor expansion in LD-LNA, showing that the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm does not converge strongly. If
\max_{1\leq k<N+1}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\to 0
, which is in line with the condition of Theorem
5.8
, the order of strong convergence is the same as that of ordinary LD using the Euler-Maruyama approximation with fixed step size
\Delta\tau
(i.e.,
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\Delta\tau})
). The proof is provided in
14.2
.

Theorem 6.4

(Strong Error)

Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{N})
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
be the respective results generated by LD (
12
) and the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm (i.e., Algorithm
1
) with the same initial condition (i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
). Suppose that Assumption
6.2
holds, we have
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{N})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})||=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\Delta\tau+\max_{1\leq k<N+1}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}}\right)
, where
\Delta\tau
is the step size in Algorithm
1
, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right)
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N
.

Theorem
6.5
shows that the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm achieves weak convergence of the same order as ordinary LD using the Euler-Maruyama approximation with fixed step size
\Delta\tau
. Notably, the Taylor expansion remainder appears only as a coefficient of
\Delta\tau
(see the proof in
14.3
). In Bayesian settings, expectations of certain functions of the model parameters, denoted as
\mathbb{E}[f(\boldsymbol{\theta})]
, are commonly used. Theorem
6.5
establishes a useful property of the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm in this context, enhancing its applicability to Bayesian learning tasks. A notable example is the posterior predictive distribution, where the function is defined as
f(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t^{*}}|\boldsymbol{\theta})
with
\boldsymbol{s}_{t^{*}}
representing the state variables at any prediction time
t^{*}
. Hence, when calculating some statistics related to
f(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm can be a good alternative to ordinary discretized LD (i.e., ULA).

Theorem 6.5

(Weak Convergence)

Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{N})
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
be the respective results generated by LD (
12
) and the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm (i.e., Algorithm
1
) with the same initial condition (i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
). Suppose that Assumption
6.2
holds, for any continuous differentiable and polynomial growth function
f
, we have
\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{N}))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}))\right]\right|=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)
, where
\Delta\tau
is the step size in Algorithm
1
.

6.3
Two-Stage Iterative Algorithm for Efficient Computation

The one-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithm (i.e., Algorithm
1
) provides a principled solution for computing the two key components in the LD-LNA framework. However, it requires evaluating both the gradient and the Hessian of the log-posterior at each iteration, resulting in a total of
N
times gradient plus
N
times Hessian calculations. This leads to a substantial computational burden, especially as the data size
M(H+1)
or the parameter dimension
N_{\theta}
increases. The cost arises from the fact that both the gradient and Hessian are derived from the explicit likelihood formulation based on the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel.

It is important to recognize that Equation (
22
) is coupled with Equation (
21
) through the parameter trajectory
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
. Notably, Equation (
21
) remains independent of the covariance term
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
appearing in Equation (
22
). This structural decoupling motivates the formulation of a two-stage solution scheme, formally introduced in Proposition
6.6
. The key distinction from the one-stage scheme lies in the treatment of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
during the iteration of Equation (
22
). In the two-stage scheme, this parameter is fixed as
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})
, as used in Equation (
26
). Consequently, the Hessian of the log-posterior is evaluated only once. Overall, the two-stage scheme computes the gradient
N_{1}
times and the Hessian once, achieving a significant gain in computational efficiency under the same termination criteria, where
N\geq N_{1}
.

Proposition 6.6

Two components
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
and
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
in LD-LNA can be obtained by first running the iterative process, i.e.,

\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1}):=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\Delta\tau

(25)

from initial value
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
for iteration steps
N_{1}
so that
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})
, and then iteratively solving

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\left[\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})}\right)^{\top}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right]\Delta\tau

(26)

from
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})
for iteration steps
N_{2}
so that
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}:=\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}+N_{2}})
, where
\Delta\tau
is the fixed step size,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are the iteration steps until some specified termination criteria are satisfied. Then we obtain the approximate LD-LNA denoted by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}+N_{2}}))
.

Building on Proposition
6.6
, we propose a two-stage iterative scheme designed to
accelerate
the RAPTOR-GEN, as outlined in Algorithm
2
. Most of the configurations and computational steps in Algorithm
2
are directly inherited from the one-stage approach in Algorithm
1
. In the first stage, we iteratively solve Equation (
25
), starting from the initial value
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
until the convergence criterion
||\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||\leq\varepsilon_{1}
is satisfied, where
\varepsilon_{1}
is a predefined tolerance. Let
N_{1}
denote the iteration step at which this condition is first met, or the maximum number of iterations if convergence is not achieved. In the second stage, using the solution from the first stage
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})
, we solve Equation (
26
) starting from
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})
. The iteration proceeds until the Frobenius norm-based convergence condition
||\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||_{\rm F}\leq\varepsilon_{2}
is satisfied, with
\varepsilon_{2}
as the predefined tolerance. The corresponding iteration step is denoted by
N_{2}
, or the maximum number of iterations if the condition is not met. The two-stage structure of Algorithm
2
offers substantial gains in computational efficiency, as evidenced by the empirical results presented in Section
7
. Further enhancements and systematic theoretical investigation are planned for future research.

Input:

The prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
, initial condition
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0),\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)\right)
, step size
\Delta\tau
, and tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
(or maximum iteration counts
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
).

Output:

Generate posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
.

Stage 1:

1.
Set the initial value
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0)
,
\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}
to a sufficiently large number, and
k:=0
;

while

\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}>\varepsilon_{1}

(or
while

k<N_{1}
)

do

2.
Calculate
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}
based on the explicit likelihood;

3.
Generate the next sample
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
through Equation (
25
);

4.
Calculate
\varepsilon_{\bar{\theta}}:=||\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||
, and set
k:=k+1
;
Stage 2:

5.
Set
N_{1}:=k
, and calculate
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}})}
based on the explicit likelihood;

6.
Set
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)
,
\varepsilon_{\Psi}
to a sufficiently large number, and
k:=N_{1}
;

while

\varepsilon_{\Psi}>\varepsilon_{2}

(or
while

\tau<N_{1}+N_{2}
)

do

7.
Generate the next sample
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
through Equation (
26
);

8.
Calculate
\varepsilon_{\Psi}:=||\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||_{\rm F}
, and set
k:=k+1
;
9.
Set
N_{2}:=k-N_{1}
, and sample
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}
from LD-LNA
\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}+N_{2}})\right)
.

Algorithm 2

Two-stage iterative algorithm for accelerating RAPTOR-GEN.

Remark 6.7

To ensure effective termination of Algorithms
1
and
2
, tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
should be set sufficiently small to indicate convergence, i.e., further iterations yield negligible improvement. These deterministic algorithms offer a key advantage over typical MCMC methods, which struggle to assess convergence during sampling. As a result, the outputs of Algorithms
1
and
2
are more stable, reliable, and reproducible. Additionally, a predefined maximum number of iterations serves as a safeguard against indefinite execution in cases of slow convergence.

Remark 6.8

Choosing the step size for discretized ODEs (i.e.,
\Delta\tau
in Equations (
21
) and (
22
) of Algorithm
1
, and Equations (
25
) and (
26
) of Algorithm
2
) is generally more straightforward than for discretized SDEs, such as the LD update in Equation (
13
). This is due to the stochastic term in SDEs, whose magnitude depends directly on the step size. An improperly chosen step size can cause parameter updates to fall outside a reasonable range, destabilizing the gradient computation of the log-posterior based on the sequential Bayesian updates in the pKG-LNA metamodel. This may result in vanishing or exploding gradients and numerical instability. As shown in Section
7
, Algorithms
1
and
2
demonstrate significantly greater robustness than discretized LD implementation (i.e., ULA).

Recall Theorem
5.10
, for Algorithms
1
and
2
, the largest eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}+N_{2}})
respectively serve as estimates of
\lambda_{\max}
, and can be used as performance indicators. To further reduce approximation error, Section
15
introduces a promising strategy that applies the Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm (MALA) as a “corrector” step following the execution of Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
. This hybrid approach enhances robustness and accuracy by leveraging MALA’s ability to refine posterior samples.

7
Empirical Study

We conduct an empirical study to validate our theoretical findings and evaluate the finite-sample performance of the proposed RAPTOR-GEN. The primary focus is on scenarios involving sparse data collection from a partially observed state. In these settings, we approximate the likelihood function using the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel. For comparison, we also present results under dense data collection, which are organized in
16.1
. Throughout the empirical analysis, we consider the algorithms: (i) ULA, the discretized LD procedure as defined in Equation (
13
), (ii) one-stage RAPTOR-GEN (i.e., Algorithm
1
), and (iii) two-stage RAPTOR-GEN (i.e., Algorithm
2
). For the step size selection, we follow a similar approach to that proposed by
Chewi et al. 2021
. As indicated by Theorem
5.10
, the dimension dependence of the LD-LNA algorithm scales as
N_{\theta}^{\frac{9}{2}}
under certain regularity conditions. Accordingly, we set the step size as
\Delta\tau=cN_{\theta}^{-\frac{9}{2}}
, where
c>0
is a tunable constant. Additionally, we assume a unit system size
\Omega:=1
, corresponding to a unit volume bioreactor in the SRN setting. Then, the molecular concentration of each species is numerically equivalent to its molecular count, simplifying the interpretation of the state variables.

7.1
Enzyme Kinetics

We begin with the enzyme kinetics model from Example
3.1
in Section
3.1
, focusing on case (i), where reaction rates follow mass-action kinetics, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(\theta_{1}s_{t}^{1}s_{t}^{2},\theta_{2}s_{t}^{3},\theta_{3}s_{t}^{3})^{\top}
. A single trajectory (
M=1
) is simulated over the interval
[0,80]
seconds using the Gillespie algorithm, with true parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\rm true}=\left(0.001,0.005,0.01\right)^{\top}
and initial states sampled from
\mathcal{N}((50,40,60,10)^{\top},\boldsymbol{I}_{4\times 4})
. The molecular counts of Enzyme (
s_{t}^{1}
), Substrate (
s_{t}^{2}
), and Complex (
s_{t}^{3}
) are treated as latent states, while only Product count
(s_{t}^{4})
is observed at intervals of
\Delta t
seconds from
t_{1}=0
to
t_{H+1}=80
, yielding
H+1
observations. Since the reaction rates do not depend directly on the Product, inference is more challenging. Observations are corrupted by additive Gaussian noise
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma)
with
\sigma=4
. For model parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(k_{F},k_{R},k_{cat})
, we assume known values for
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
(Reactions 1 and 2), and aim to infer the unknown kinetic parameter
\theta_{3}
(Reaction 3) and the noise level
\sigma
. This setup reflects real-world enzymatic studies, where the focus is often on estimating the enzyme turnover rate
k_{\rm cat}

(
Wang et al. 2024b
)
. To evaluate algorithm performance under high model uncertainty, we consider three sparse data regimes
H=4,8,16
(with
\Delta t=20,10,5
seconds, respectively). The priors are specified as
\theta_{3}\sim U(0,1)
and
\sigma\sim U(0,25)
with a posterior sample size of
B=100
.

Figure
7.1
illustrates that the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel recovers well the dynamics of three completely unobserved latent state variables and one partially observed and noisy state variable, demonstrating its promising capabilities in trajectory inference. In addition, the inferred trajectories provide better fits to the true trajectories as the data size increases. Note that the jumps in the inferred trajectories are due to the fact that the LNA metamodel is updated based on the available observations at each observation collection time, sequentially correcting the approximation error.

(unobserved).

s_{t}^{2}
(unobserved).

(c)

s_{t}^{3}
(unobserved).

(d)

s_{t}^{4}
(partially observed).

Trajectory inference for four state variables by Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel with three data sizes.
The red curves represent true trajectories of four state variables simulated by Gillespie algorithm. The blue curves and shaded areas represent the means and 95% prediction intervals of 1000 inferred trajectories obtained by Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel based on the limited and noisy observations shown via the magenta square dots.

Table
7.1
presents the root mean square errors (RMSEs), computed from
B
posterior samples, to quantify the differences between the estimated and the true parameters, along with estimates of
\lambda_{\max}
. These results indicate that the one- and two-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithms yield equivalent parameter estimates. Both algorithms use tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=1\times 10^{-5}
. The columns
N
and
N_{1}
show that the one-stage RAPTOR-GEN performs
N
gradient and
N
Hessian evaluations, while the two-stage version performs
N_{1}
gradient evaluations and only one Hessian evaluation. Combined with the runtimes
T_{\rm one-stage}
and
T_{\rm two-stage}
, the results demonstrate that the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN significantly improves computational efficiency.

To demonstrate that RAPTOR-GEN overcomes the step size sensitivity issue faced by ULA, we evaluate both methods using step sizes
c=1,0.75,0.5,0.25
. For ULA, we set a warm-up length of
N_{0}=1000
to reduce initial bias and
\delta=10
to mitigate sample correlation. Table
7.1
summarizes the results of parameter inference across 100 randomly selected macro-replications. Two columns of Solved Number, together with parameter RMSEs, show that the performance of ULA is very sensitive to the choice of step size, and RAPTOR-GEN is more robust. Comparing
T_{\rm ULA}
with
T_{\rm two-stage}
, RAPTOR-GEN shows a significant improvement in computational efficiency over ULA. The confidence intervals (CIs) of the parameter RMSEs also reveal that the two parameters inferred by ULA, especially
\sigma
, are far from convergent with the initial warm-up length
N_{0}=1000
. And the estimates of
\lambda_{\max}
that decrease as data size increases are consistent with the finite-sample performance proposed in Theorem
5.10
and Corollary
5.11
.

Finally, because RAPTOR-GEN is a likelihood-based method, we assess its strengths and limitations by comparing its performance against a widely used likelihood-free approach—the ABC-SMC sampler
(
Sisson et al. 2007
)
. This comparison underscores the advantages of leveraging explicit likelihood information over relying solely on likelihood-free sampling. The detailed methodology and results of this evaluation are provided in Appendix
16.2.2
. Our findings demonstrate that RAPTOR-GEN achieves more accurate parameter estimation, particularly for the measurement error level, by fully exploiting the explicit likelihood derived from a moderately sized dataset. Furthermore, the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN exhibits substantially faster convergence than ABC-SMC, especially as the dataset size increases.

7.2
Simplified Prokaryotic Autoregulation Gene Network

To assess the empirical performance of the proposed RAPTOR-GEN in scenarios with multi-dimensional unknown kinetic rate parameters, we use a simplified prokaryotic autoregulation gene network example involving five biochemical species, i.e., DNA, RNA, protein (denoted by P), protein dimer (denoted by P
2
), and DNA
\cdot
P
2
. The self-regulating production of the protein P and its dimer P
2
can be represented by the following eight chemical reactions,

\displaystyle\text{Reaction }1:\ {\rm DNA}+{\rm P}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{1}}{\rm DNA}\cdot{\rm P}_{2},\quad\text{Reaction }2:\ {\rm DNA}\cdot{\rm P}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{2}}{\rm DNA}+{\rm P}_{2},

\displaystyle\text{Reaction }3:\ {\rm DNA}\xrightarrow{v_{3}}{\rm DNA}+{\rm RNA},\quad\ \ \text{Reaction }4:\ {\rm RNA}\xrightarrow{v_{4}}{\rm RNA}+{\rm P},

\displaystyle\text{Reaction }5:\ 2{\rm P}\xrightarrow{v_{5}}{\rm P}_{2},\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \ \text{Reaction }6:\ {\rm P}_{2}\xrightarrow{v_{6}}2{\rm P},

\displaystyle\text{Reaction }7:\ {\rm RNA}\xrightarrow{v_{7}}\emptyset,\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \text{Reaction }8:\ {\rm P}\xrightarrow{v_{8}}\emptyset.

Reactions 1 and 2 describe the reversible process in which the protein dimer P
2
binds to DNA, and thereby inhibits the production of the protein P shown by Reactions 3 and 4. The reversible dimerization of the protein is characterized by Reactions 5 and 6, while Reactions 7 and 8 complete the degradation of the RNA and of the protein. The system has been analyzed by
Golightly and Wilkinson 2005
, and therefore we adopt the same settings to aid the exposition. In particular, the total molecular count of DNA and DNA
\cdot
P
2
, i.e.,
\zeta
(a.k.a. conservation constant), is fixed throughout the evolution of the system. Thus, we can substitute
\zeta-{\rm DNA}
for DNA
\cdot
P
2
to reduce the model to one involving only four biochemical species, i.e., DNA, RNA, P, and P
2
, and the system state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=(s_{t}^{1},s_{t}^{2},s_{t}^{3},s_{t}^{4})^{\top}
includes the respective molecular counts of DNA, RNA, P, and P
2
at any time
t
. The stoichiometry matrix associated with the reduced system is
\boldsymbol{C}
with
C_{11}=C_{27}=C_{38}=C_{41}=C_{46}=-1
,
C_{12}=C_{23}=C_{34}=C_{42}=C_{45}=1
,
C_{35}=-2
,
C_{36}=2
, and zeros elsewhere. We use mass-action kinetics to model reaction rates as
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(\theta_{1}s_{t}^{1}s_{t}^{4},\theta_{2}(\zeta-s_{t}^{1}),\theta_{3}s_{t}^{1},\theta_{4}s_{t}^{2},0.5\theta_{5}s_{t}^{3}(s_{t}^{3}-1),\theta_{6}s_{t}^{4},\theta_{7}s_{t}^{2},\theta_{8}s_{t}^{3})^{\top}
. Our goal is to infer the unknown kinetic rate parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{8})^{\top}
.

We simulate a single measurement trajectory (
M=1
) over the interval
[0,50]
using the Gillespie algorithm with true parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\rm true}=(0.1,0.7,0.35,0.2,0.1,0.9,0.3,0.1)^{\top}
and initial states
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{1}}\sim\mathcal{N}((5,5,5,5)^{\top},\boldsymbol{I}_{4\times 4})
. Due to the difficulty of directly measuring DNA activation states, we treat the DNA count (
s_{t}^{1}
) as a latent state, while RNA, P, and P
2
counts (
s_{t}^{2}
,
s_{t}^{3}
, and
s_{t}^{4}
) are observed every
\Delta t=0.5
from
t_{1}=0
to
t_{H+1}=50
, yielding
H+1=101
observations. In this partially observed system, DNA and DNA
\cdot
P
2
are unobserved, but the conservation constant
\zeta=10
is known, representing the gene copy number, which is typically observable in practice. Figure
7.2
demonstrates that the inferred trajectories from the Bayesian pKG-LNA metamodel closely match the true dynamics, effectively recovering the system behavior including the fully unobserved latent state
s_{t}^{1}
.

To assess RAPTOR-GEN’s inference performance in scenario with moderately high-dimensional parameter spaces, we benchmark it against ULA, using a carefully chosen step size of
c=45
. For RAPTOR-GEN, we set tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=1\times 10^{-6}
and record its computational budget. ULA is then run with the same budget, using
\delta=10
to reduce the sample correlation. Both methods generate
B=100
posterior samples, with uniform priors
\theta_{k}\sim U(0.1,0.9)
for
k=1,\ldots,8
. Table
7.2
summarizes the results. RAPTOR-GEN yields substantially lower RMSEs across most parameters compared to ULA, with similar performance only for
\theta_{7}
. Notably, ULA struggles to converge for parameters such as
\theta_{2}
and
\theta_{6}
, as indicated by wide RMSE CIs. RAPTOR-GEN demonstrates superior computational efficiency and accurately recovers system parameters, except for
\theta_{2}
, which appears only in the equation for the unobserved latent state
s_{t}^{1}
. Combined with Figure
7.2
, these results show that RAPTOR-GEN effectively reconstructs the whole system, even with completely unobserved latent dynamics.

8
Concluding Remarks

This paper tackles key challenges in biomanufacturing system management, where mechanisms are poorly understood and data are sparse, noisy, and heterogeneous. To advance scientific understanding and accelerate digital twin development, we introduce RAPTOR-GEN, a mechanism-informed Bayesian learning framework featuring an interpretable metamodel and efficient posterior sampling. RAPTOR-GEN is built upon a modular, multi-scale pKG mechanistic foundational model for Bio-SoS, formulated as a system of SDEs. It flexibly captures spatial-temporal causal interdependencies across molecular, cellular, and macroscopic scales, enabling representation of diverse biomanufacturing processes. The metamodel, pKG-LNA, applies the linear noise approximation (LNA) to the underlying SDE dynamics and incorporates a sequential learning strategy to integrate sparse, noisy measurements, enabling inference of latent states and derivation of a closed-form likelihood. To improve sample efficiency and interpretability, LD-LNA leverages Langevin diffusion (LD) to quantify parameter interdependencies and accelerate posterior sampling. By generalizing LNA, we avoid step size tuning in LD and derive a finite-sample performance bound for the approximation error. We also present a practical RAPTOR-GEN algorithm for fast and robust posterior sampling with provable error control. Numerical experiments validate the framework’s effectiveness in learning mechanistic parameters and quantifying uncertainty in biomanufacturing processes.

This study offers three potential avenues for extension. First, while our focus is on mechanistic learning and digital twin platform development for biomanufacturing systems, the proposed SDE-based pKG model and sequential Bayesian learning framework are broadly applicable to other domains in operations research and operations management, such as queueing systems and financial modeling. Second, the proposed Bayesian learning framework based on likelihood gradients can be extended to incorporate higher-order information, enabling the capture of complex parameter interactions and facilitating model structure selection to support more sample-efficient recovery of the underlying mechanisms. Third, LD-LNA within the proposed posterior sampling framework offers a fast and robust implementation procedure that enhances mixing and mitigates the challenge of step size selection. As a result, LD-LNA emerges as a promising alternative to the commonly used LD in the reverse process of diffusion models.

Acknowledgments.
We gratefully acknowledge funding support from the National Institute of Standards and Technology (Grant nos. 70NANB24H293) and the National Science Foundation (Grant CAREER CMMI-2442970).

= diag
(x,x,\ldots,x)

10
Gibbs Sampling for Model Uncertainty Quantification on SRN

This section derives a Gibbs sampling procedure for SRN model parameters under dense data collection with ignorable measurement error so that the observations
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}:=\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
for
h=1,2,\ldots,H+1
, and we use Equation (
3
) for the state transition density. First, let

\displaystyle G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})

\displaystyle=s_{t_{h}}^{j}+\boldsymbol{C}^{j}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}})\Delta{t_{h}}=s_{t_{h}}^{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{r}}C_{jl}v_{l}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{l})\Delta{t_{h}}

\displaystyle=s_{t_{h}}^{j}+\sum_{l=1,l\neq k}^{N_{r}}C_{jl}v_{l}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{l})\Delta{t_{h}}+C_{jk}v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k,-n})\Delta{t_{h}},

\displaystyle G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{C}^{j}{\rm diag}\{v_{l}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{l})\}\boldsymbol{C}^{j\top}\Delta{t_{h}}=\sum_{l=1,l\neq k}^{N_{r}}C_{jl}^{2}v_{l}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{l})\Delta{t_{h}}+C_{jk}^{2}v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k,-n})\Delta{t_{h}},

where
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}
represents the
n
-th component of the
N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}
-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k}
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k,-n}
represents the collection of parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k}
excluding the
n
-th component,
s_{t_{h}}^{j}
represents the
j
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}
, and
\boldsymbol{C}^{j}
and
C_{jk}
represent the
j
-th row and the
(j,k)
-th element of the stoichiometry matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
, respectively. From Equation (
3
), we have

s_{t_{h+1}}^{j}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}),G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\right).

(27)

Now we derive the conditional posterior for each parameter in
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}
. Given the data
\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M}
and the collection of parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}
excluding the
n
-th component of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{k}
denoted by
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}
, we can infer
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}={G_{2}^{j}}^{-1}(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}))
as follows, where
{G_{2}^{j}}^{-1}
is the inverse function of
G_{2}^{j}
. Since the conditional posterior cumulative distribution function (CDF) for the random variable
\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}
is

\displaystyle\quad F_{\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}\left(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\leq\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbb{P}\left(G_{2}^{j}(\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\leq G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right),\ {\scriptsize\text{if }G_{2}^{j}\text{ is monotonically nondecreasing with respect to }\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}\\
1-\mathbb{P}\left(G_{2}^{j}(\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\leq G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right),\ {\scriptsize\text{otherwise}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}F_{G_{2}^{j}(\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right),\ {\scriptsize\text{if }G_{2}^{j}\text{ is monotonically nondecreasing with respect to }\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}\\
1-F_{G_{2}^{j}(\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right),\ {\scriptsize\text{otherwise}}\end{cases}

(28)

Then the conditional posterior PDF is

p\left(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right)=\frac{d}{d\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}F_{\Theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}\left(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right).

(29)

According to Equation (
28
), we need to infer the function of parameters, i.e.,
G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
. To obtain the posterior distribution of
G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
, we first derive the joint posterior distribution for
G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
and
G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
. Without strong prior information and for simplicity, we consider the conjugate prior with initial hyperparameters giving relatively flat density, that is, a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})|G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n});G_{1,0}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}),\frac{G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{\kappa_{0}})\times
inverted-chi square Inv-
\chi(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n});\psi_{0},G_{2,0}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}))
prior. The Gaussian likelihood (
27
) combined with the above prior can yield a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})|G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n});G_{1,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}),\frac{G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{\kappa_{M}})\times
inverted-chi square Inv-
\chi(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n});\psi_{M},G_{2,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}))
joint posterior distribution where

\displaystyle G_{1,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})=\frac{1}{\kappa_{M}}\left(\kappa_{0}G_{1,0}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})+M\bar{s}_{t_{h+1}}^{j}\right)\text{ with }\kappa_{M}=\kappa_{0}+M,\ \bar{s}_{t_{h+1}}^{j}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}y_{t_{h+1}}^{j,(i)},

\displaystyle G_{2,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})=\frac{\psi_{0}G_{2,0}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})+(M-1)\sigma_{t_{h+1},j}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{0}M}{\kappa_{M}}\left(G_{1,0}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})-\bar{s}_{t_{h+1}}^{j}\right)^{2}}{\psi_{M}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{with }\psi_{M}=\psi_{0}+M,\ \sigma_{t_{h+1},j}^{2}=\frac{1}{M-1}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left(y_{t_{h+1}}^{j,(i)}-\bar{s}_{t_{h+1}}^{j}\right)^{2}.

where
y_{t_{h+1}}^{j,(i)}
is the
j
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}
. Then, we can obtain the marginal posterior distribution for
G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
by integrating out
G_{1}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
from the above joint posterior as follows,

p\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right)\propto\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\right)^{-\left(\frac{\psi_{M}}{2}+1\right)}\exp\left\{-\frac{\psi_{M}G_{2,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{2G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}\right\}.

Combined with Equation (
29
), we finally obtain the conditional posterior distribution for
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}
:

\displaystyle\quad p\left(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}\bigg|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{h}}^{-(k,n)}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\begin{cases}\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\right)^{-\left(\frac{\psi_{M}}{2}+1\right)}\exp\left\{-\frac{\psi_{M}G_{2,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{2G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}\right\}\frac{dG_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{d\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}},\ {\scriptsize\text{if }G_{2}^{j}\text{ is monotonically nondecreasing with respect to }\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}}\\
-\left(G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})\right)^{-\left(\frac{\psi_{M}}{2}+1\right)}\exp\left\{-\frac{\psi_{M}G_{2,M}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{2G_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}\right\}\frac{dG_{2}^{j}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})}{d\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}},\ {\scriptsize\text{otherwise}}\end{cases}

(30)

Based on the conditional posterior distributions derived above, we provide the Gibbs sampling procedure in Algorithm
3
to generate posterior samples. We first set the prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and generate the initial point
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
by sampling the prior. Within each
r
-th iteration of the Gibbs sampling, given the previous sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(r-1)}
, we sequentially compute and generate one sample from the conditional posterior distribution for each parameter
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}
. Repetition of this procedure can get samples
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(r)}
for
r=1,2,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta+1
. Note that to reduce initial bias and correlations between consecutive samples, we remove the first
N_{0}
samples and keep one for every
\delta
samples. Consequently, we obtain the posterior samples
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(b)}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
with
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

Remark 10.1

The techniques used in Algorithm
3
to reduce correlations in Gibbs samples include discarding the first
N_{0}
steps, called burn-in iterations, and subsampling the chain using only every
\delta
step, also called thinning. However, in general, the use of thinning decreases the statistical efficiency of the Gibbs estimator
(
MacEachern and Berliner 1994
)
.

Input:

The prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}=\{\{(\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M}\}_{h=1}^{H}
, posterior sample size
B
, initial warm-up length
N_{0}
, and an appropriate integer
\delta
to reduce sample correlation.

Output:

Posterior samples
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(b)}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
with
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

1.
Set the initial value
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
by sampling prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
;

for

r=1,2,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta+1

do

for

h=1,2,\ldots,H

do

for

k=1,2,\ldots,{N_{r}}

do

2.
Choose
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,N_{s}\}
that satisfies
C_{jk}\neq 0
;

for

n=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}

do

3.
Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}:=\bigg\{

\displaystyle\{\theta_{t_{h_{0}}}^{k_{0},n_{0},(r)},h_{0}\in[h-1],k_{0}\in[N_{r}],n_{0}\in[N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}]\},

\displaystyle\{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k_{0},n_{0},(r)},k_{0}\in[k-1],n_{0}\in[N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}]\},\{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n_{0},(r)},n_{0}\in[n-1]\},

\displaystyle\{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n_{0},(r-1)},n_{0}\in\{n+1,\ldots,N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}\}\},

\displaystyle\{\theta_{t_{h_{0}}}^{k_{0},n_{0},(r-1)},h_{0}\in\{h+1,\ldots,H\},k_{0}\in\{k+1,\ldots,N_{r}\},n_{0}\in[N_{\theta_{t_{h}}^{k}}]\}\bigg\};

4.
Set
G_{1,0}^{j,(r)}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
,
\kappa_{0}^{(r)}
,
\psi_{0}^{(r)}
and
G_{2,0}^{j,(r)}(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n})
according to
\mathcal{X}
and
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n,(r-1)}
;

5.
Generate
\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n,(r)}\sim p(\theta_{t_{h}}^{k,n}|\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{M},\mathcal{X})
through Equation (
30
);

6.
Obtain a new posterior sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(r)}
;
7.
Return
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(b)}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(N_{0}+(b-1)\delta+1)}
for
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

Algorithm 3

Gibbs sampler for model parameters of SRN.

11
Supplements to LNA Metamodel

11.1
Some Theoretical Results about LNA for SRN.

Recall that
\Omega
is a size parameter of the system, and the relation between molecular concentrations
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
and molecular counts
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}
is
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}
. We impose the following Assumption
11.1
(adopted from Assumption 1 in
Ferm et al. 2008
).

{assumption}

The propensity functions have continuous third derivatives in space and time, i.e.,
\omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\in C^{3}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\geq 0,t\geq 0\})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{r}
, and the relation between the propensity functions and the reaction rates (i.e.,
v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{r}
) can be written as
\omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})=\Omega v_{k}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)=\Omega v_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
.

Suppose that Assumption
11.1
holds. If the initial conditions
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
(the molecular counts of species at the initial time
t=0
) satisfy
\lim_{\Omega\to\infty}\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{0}=\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{0}
where
\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{0}
is the mean vector of the LNA model at the initial time
t=0
, then a result similar to the law of large numbers has been shown in
Kurtz 1970
, that is,

\lim_{\Omega\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\leq T}||\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}-\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}||>\epsilon_{0}\right)=0,

for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
in a finite time interval
t\in[0,T]
. This means that for a large system size
\Omega
, the molecular concentrations of species are well approximated by the mean vector of the LNA model. Moreover, consider a shifted and scaled version of
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}
defined by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}=\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}-\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}\right)
. In
Kurtz 1971
, it has been shown that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}
is an estimate similar to CLT, that is,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}
converges to a Gaussian random vector with mean
\boldsymbol{0}
and a covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}
in a finite interval
t\in[0,T]
when
\Omega\to\infty
. Furthermore, a recent work provides nonasymptotic bounds as functions of the system size
\Omega
on the error between the LNA and the stationary distribution of an appropriately scaled SRN; see
Grunberg and Del Vecchio 2023
for details.

11.2
More Descriptions of Bayesian Updating pKG-LNA Metamodel

The procedure of Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel is summarized in Figure
11.2
.

\FIGURE

The procedure of Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel.

12
ULA for Bayesian Posterior Sampling

We provide a detailed ULA procedure in Algorithm
4
to generate posterior samples for the parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. We first set the prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and the initial value
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
. The initial value can be set arbitrarily or generated by sampling the prior. Within each iteration of the ULA posterior sampler, given the previous sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
, we compute and generate a proposal sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
from the discretized LD (
13
). In particular, the calculation of the gradient of log-posterior in Step 2 is based on the explicit derivation of likelihood, which can be built on the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel discussed in Section
4.3
in situations with sparse data collection, or the state transition model (
3
) in situations with dense data collection. Repetition of this procedure can get samples
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
for
K=1,2,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta+1
. Note that to reduce initial bias and correlations between consecutive samples, we remove the first
N_{0}
samples and keep one for every
\delta
samples. Consequently, we obtain the posterior samples
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
with
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

Input:

The prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
, step size
\Delta\tau
, posterior sample size
B
, initial warm-up length
N_{0}
, and an appropriate integer
\delta
to reduce sample correlation.

Output:

Posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
.

1.
Set the initial value
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
;

for

k=0,1,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta

do

2.
Calculate
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}
based on the explicit derivation of likelihood;

3.
Generate
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
through Equation (
13
);
4.
Return
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{0}+(b-1)\delta+1})
for
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

Algorithm 4

ULA posterior sampler for parameters of SRN.

13
Proofs of Theorems, Propositions and Corollaries in Sections
4
and
5

This section provides the proofs of Theorem
5.1
, Proposition
4.1
, Theorem
5.4
, Theorem
5.8
, Theorem
5.10
, and Corollary
5.11
of the paper.

13.1
Proof of Proposition
4.1

Proof 13.1

Proof.
Define
\boldsymbol{G}_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}]
. Recall
\boldsymbol{m}_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{M}_{t}]
and
\boldsymbol{K}_{t}=\text{Var}[\boldsymbol{M}_{t}]=\boldsymbol{G}_{t}-\boldsymbol{m}_{t}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}
. Then from Equation (
9
), we have

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{m}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}dt\right]=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}dt,

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{G}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{M}_{t+dt}\boldsymbol{M}_{t+dt}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{M}_{t}d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}+\left(d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}+d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}d\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}\boldsymbol{M}_{t}^{\top}\right]dt+\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\right]dt

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{G}_{t}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)dt,

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{K}_{t}

\displaystyle=d\boldsymbol{G}_{t}-d\left(\boldsymbol{m}_{t}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}\right)=d\boldsymbol{G}_{t}-\boldsymbol{m}_{t}d\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}-\left(d\boldsymbol{m}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{G}_{t}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\boldsymbol{m}_{t}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}\boldsymbol{m}_{t}^{\top}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{t}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{a}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}}\boldsymbol{K}_{t}+\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)\boldsymbol{S}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)dt.

\square

13.2
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Proof 13.2

Proof.
This can be proved by using the Fokker-Planck equation
(
Risken and Frank 1996
)
. Recall that
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is an
N_{\theta}
-dimensional vector. The Fokker-Planck equation is a partial differential equation describing the time evolution of the PDF of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
at time
\tau
denoted by
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)
given by

\partial_{\tau}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau),

(31)

where
\theta_{k}
is the
k
-th component of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
. As
\tau\to\infty
,
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\to q(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. And since
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we can interchange the order of limit and derivative. Thus we have
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\partial_{\tau}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=\partial_{\tau}\lim_{\tau\to\infty}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=\partial_{\tau}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, and

\displaystyle\quad\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\left\{-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left\{\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\partial_{\theta_{k}}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left\{-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)+\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\}=0.

Hence, for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, we have
