-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)+\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log q(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, which yields the conclusion
q(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\exp\left\{\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right\}=p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
.
\square

13.3
Proof of Theorem
5.4

The mean
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)
of
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
can be obtained by Proposition
13.3
, which is directly from Proposition
4.1
. In particular, let the initial value
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(0):=\boldsymbol{0}
, then
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
\tau\geq 0
according to the ODE (
32
).

Proposition 13.3

The mean vector
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)
and the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)
of the Gaussian variable
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
for any
\tau\geq 0
can be obtained by solving the ODEs in (
32
) and (
33
),

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\tau)d\tau,

(32)

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\tau)+2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)d\tau,

(33)

with initial values
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(0)
, where
\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}
is an
N_{\theta}
-by-
N_{\theta}
identity matrix.

The mean
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)
of
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
can be obtained by Proposition
13.4
, which is directly from Proposition
13.3
. Likewise, let the initial value
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(0):=\boldsymbol{0}
, then
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
\tau\geq 0
according to the ODE (
34
).

Proposition 13.4

The mean vector
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)
and the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)
of the Gaussian variable
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\tau)
for any
\tau\geq 0
can be obtained by solving the ODEs in (
34
) and (
35
),

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\tau)d\tau,

(34)

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\tau)+2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)d\tau,

(35)

with initial values
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(0)
, where
\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}
is an
N_{\theta}
-by-
N_{\theta}
identity matrix.

To prove Theorem
5.4
, we need the following Lemma
13.5
.

Lemma 13.5

Suppose that Assumption
5.2
holds,
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
is Hurwitz, which means that all of its eigenvalues have strictly negative real parts.

Proof 13.6

Proof.
Note that
\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
attains an isolated local maximum at
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
, which means that the Hessian matrix
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is negative definite at
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
. And since
\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is three times differentiable, its second partial derivatives are all continuous, thus the Hessian matrix
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
is a symmetric matrix by the symmetry of the second derivatives. Hence, the real symmetric matrix
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
is negative definite if and only if all of its eigenvalues are negative, which means it is Hurwitz.
\square

Now we provide the proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Proof 13.7

Proof.
From Proposition
13.4
, we have
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)
is a zero mean Gaussian with covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}
given as the solution to the following equation,

\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{\top}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)=-2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}.

(36)

We note that Equation (
36
) is a Lyapunov equation and organize the following lemma from
Simoncini 2016
,

Lemma 13.8

Given any square matrices
\boldsymbol{A}
,
\boldsymbol{P}
,
\boldsymbol{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, where
\boldsymbol{P}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}
are symmetric, and
\boldsymbol{Q}
is positive definite, there exists a unique positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
satisfying
\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\boldsymbol{P}+\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{A}+\boldsymbol{Q}=\boldsymbol{0}_{n\times n}
if and only if the linear system
\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}
is globally asymptotically stable, that is,
\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)\to 0
as
\tau\to\infty
.

And if
\boldsymbol{A}
is a Hurwitz matrix, which can also be called a stable matrix, then the differential equation
\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}
is asymptotically stable. Hence, Equation (
36
) has a unique positive definite solution as long as
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
is Hurwitz. This condition holds in this case due to Lemma
13.5
. Furthermore, we can obtain this unique positive definite solution
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)=(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
.

\square

13.4
Proof of Theorem
5.8

First, we derive the Taylor series for the
j
-th component of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}
. Specifically, for each
j
-th component of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}
, i.e.,
\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}
, there exists
\alpha_{j}\in[0,1]
such that
\alpha_{j}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right)+(1-\alpha_{j})\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
, and for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)^{\top}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right)^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\left[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\mathcal{O}_{j}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau).

The Lagrange form of the remainder
R_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))=\left(\mathcal{O}_{j}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
is the
j
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))=\left(\mathcal{O}(||\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)||)\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
, with
\mathcal{O}_{j}(1)
a matrix equal to
\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)}
.

Now we provide the proof of Theorem
5.8
.

Proof 13.9

Proof.
For theoretical use, we represent Equation (
15
) using the Euler-Maruyama approximation with a fixed step size
\Delta\tau>0
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right\}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau).

According to Taylor expansion (
19
), the above equation can be represented as follows,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right\}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau).

(37)

Let
\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})
be the characteristic function of
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)
(the PDF of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
at time
\tau
), defined by

\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})=\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau).

(38)

The characteristic function of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau
is

\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau}(\boldsymbol{v})=\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau\right\}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau).

And the characteristic function of
-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\Delta\tau
is

\displaystyle\phi_{-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\Delta\tau}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left\{-i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\Delta\tau\right\}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(-i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\Delta\tau\right)^{l}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(-i\Delta\tau\right)^{l}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right)^{l}\right]=1-\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2}).

It is known that the characteristic function of
\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
is
\phi_{\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})=\exp\left\{-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right\}
since
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\text{diag}\{\Delta\tau\}\right)
. Hence, the characteristic function of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)
is

\displaystyle\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau}(\boldsymbol{v})\phi_{-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\Delta\tau}(\boldsymbol{v})\phi_{\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau\right\}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left(1-\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})\right)\exp\left\{-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right\}

\displaystyle=\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left(1-\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2}).

(39)

From Equations (
38
) and (
39
), and the fact that
\exp(x)=1+x+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)}(\boldsymbol{v})-\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right\}\left(1-\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\left(1+i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})\right)\left(1-\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle-\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(1+i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]

\displaystyle\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\Delta\tau-\Delta\tau\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle-\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\Delta\tau i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{3}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)}(\boldsymbol{v})-\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\Delta\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle-\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(iv_{k}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}-v_{k}^{2}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle-\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(iv_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)^{2}\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)\int\exp\left\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)\right\}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\,d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2}),

where
R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))=\left(\mathcal{O}_{k}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)
is the
k
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the Fourier transform defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left[f(\boldsymbol{x})\right](\boldsymbol{v})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int f(\boldsymbol{x})\exp\{i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}\}\,d\boldsymbol{x},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[f(\boldsymbol{x})\right](\boldsymbol{v})=f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int\mathcal{F}\left[f(\boldsymbol{x})\right](\boldsymbol{v})\exp\{-i\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}\}\,d\boldsymbol{v},

for an integrable function
f
. And the Fourier transform of the derivatives with respect to
x_{k}
of the
l
-th order
\partial_{x_{k}}^{l}f(\boldsymbol{x})
is
\mathcal{F}\left[\partial_{x_{k}}^{l}f(\boldsymbol{x})\right](\boldsymbol{v})=(-iv_{k})^{l}\mathcal{F}\left[f(\boldsymbol{x})\right](\boldsymbol{v})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau+1)}(\boldsymbol{v})-\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Delta\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)\mathcal{F}\left[\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right](\boldsymbol{v})+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)^{2}\mathcal{F}\left[p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right](\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(-iv_{k}\right)\mathcal{F}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right](\boldsymbol{v})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathcal{F}\left[\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)\right](\boldsymbol{v})+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathcal{F}\left[\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right](\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathcal{F}\left[\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)\right](\boldsymbol{v})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2}).

From the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{v})=\sqrt{2\pi}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau+1)-p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)}{\Delta\tau}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau^{2}).

Now, letting
\Delta\tau\to 0
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=\lim_{\Delta\tau\rightarrow 0}\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau+1)-p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)}{\Delta\tau}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right).

Further, let
q(\boldsymbol{\theta})
be the stationary distribution of
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)
. As
\tau\to\infty
,
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\to q(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. And since
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we can interchange the order of limit and derivative. Thus, we have
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\partial_{\tau}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=\partial_{\tau}\lim_{\tau\to\infty}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)=\partial_{\tau}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
. And by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we can interchange the order of limit and integration. That is,
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]
(by the fact that
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)
). Thus, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}

\displaystyle\left\{-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}\right)p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\right]p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\left(\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}^{2}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}\left\{\left(-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right)q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\partial_{\theta_{k}}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta_{k}}q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left\{-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)+\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]\right\}=0,

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{O}_{k}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\text{tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty){\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty){\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right]+\text{Cov}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty),\mathcal{O}_{k}(1)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}(\infty){\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}}^{\top}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)+\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right).

(40)

From Theorem
5.4
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]=\text{tr}\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right)
. And by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\left[\text{tr}\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\text{tr}\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-1}\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\top}\right)\text{tr}\left(\mathcal{O}_{k}(1)\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\text{tr}\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-1}\right)\right]^{2}\left[\text{tr}\left(\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\top}(1)\right)\right]^{2}.

Since tr
(\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}(\infty))=\text{tr}\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-1}\right)\to 0
, by the squeeze theorem we obtain
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}(\infty))\right]\to 0
. Thus, for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, we have
-\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)+\partial_{\theta_{k}}\log q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\to 0
, which yields the conclusion that
q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\to\exp\left\{\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\right\}=p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
, meaning that the stationary distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
converges to the target posterior distribution
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
.
\square

13.5
Proof of Theorem
5.10

First, we convert the multivariate Gaussian conditional distribution
p(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})
in Section
4.3
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}})
, to the canonical exponential family form via reparameterization.

Example 13.10

We can rewrite
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}},\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}})
as an exponential family distribution in the canonical form where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta})=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}}\\
{\rm vec}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}}\right)^{-1}\right]\end{pmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}})=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}\\
{\rm vec}\left[\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}\right]\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}};\boldsymbol{\theta}))=\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\left|\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}}\right|\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\left(\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}}\right)^{\top}\left(\Omega^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t_{h+1}}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t_{h+1}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{t_{h+1}}\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t_{h+1}}\right),\quad g(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}})=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|\log(2\pi),

where vec
[\cdot]
is the vectorized operation for an
n
-by-
m
matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=(a_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n,m}
simply stacking the rows one at a time to create an
nm
-dimensional vector, i.e.,
{\rm vec}[\boldsymbol{A}]=(a_{11},\ldots,a_{1m},\ldots,a_{n1},\ldots,a_{nm})^{\top}
.

Next, since the likelihood function of historical observations
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
can be written as

\displaystyle p\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\big|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{M}\left\{p\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}\big|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\prod_{h=1}^{H}p\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}\big|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\right]-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}g(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\sum_{h=1}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))\right]\right\},

and according to Bayes’ rule, we have the posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
given historical observations
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
as follows,

\displaystyle p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta})p\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\big|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\int_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}p(\boldsymbol{\theta})p\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\big|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\sum_{h=1}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))\right]+\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\right\},

where

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\exp

\displaystyle\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)})+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\sum_{h=1}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))\right]+\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\right\}\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}

is the normalization constant. In the following discussions, for the sake of notational simplicity, we set
\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{0}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})=\eta_{k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,K_{1}
,
i=1,2,\ldots,M
, and
\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{M}[A(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\sum_{h=1}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))]-\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))-\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)
. Then, the above posterior distribution can be rewritten as

p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)=\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\},

(41)

where
\rho(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\}\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

The main tool used to derive the bound is the Stein method. Therefore, we first give the definition of the Stein operator, which is synthesized from studies
Anastasiou et al. 2023
,
Barbour 1990
,
Gotze 1991
.

Definition 13.11

(Stein Density Operator)

For a random vector
\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with PDF
p
, a Stein density operator is given by

T_{\boldsymbol{W}}f(\boldsymbol{w})=\Delta f(\boldsymbol{w})+<\nabla\log p(\boldsymbol{w}),\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w})>,

(42)

where
\Delta
is the Laplacian and
<\cdot,\cdot>
is the scalar product. Moreover, for the
d
-dimensional standard multivariate Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{d\times d}\right)
, we have

T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f(\boldsymbol{w})=\Delta f(\boldsymbol{w})-<\boldsymbol{w},\nabla f(\boldsymbol{w})>.

(43)

Next, according to
Braverman et al. 2017
, we give the definition of the Stein equation for the standard multivariate Gaussian distribution, which is the center part of the Stein method.

Definition 13.12

(Standard Gaussian Stein Equation)

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{d\times d}\right)
and
h
be a suitable function. The Stein equation for the standard multivariate Gaussian distribution is

T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})=\Delta f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})-<\boldsymbol{w},\nabla f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})>=h(\boldsymbol{w})-\mathbb{E}\left[h(\boldsymbol{Z})\right].

(44)

To prove Theorem
5.10
, we need the following Lemma
13.13
from Lemma 4.2 in
Grunberg and Del Vecchio 2023
, which reformulates the 1-Wasserstein distance computed by taking a supremum over the 1-Lipschitz functions into the 1-Lipschitz functions that are also in
C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with bounded derivatives.

Lemma 13.13

The following holds for any two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{W}_{1}}|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{X}\sim\mathbb{P}}[h(\boldsymbol{X})]-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{Y}\sim\mathbb{Q}}[h(\boldsymbol{Y})]|=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{X}\sim\mathbb{P}}[h(\boldsymbol{X})]-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{Y}\sim\mathbb{Q}}[h(\boldsymbol{Y})]|,

where
\mathcal{H}=\{h\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ |\ h\in\mathcal{W}_{1},\forall k\in\{1,2,3\},\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}||\nabla^{k}h(\boldsymbol{x})||<\infty\}
.

We also need the following derivative bound, which is adopted from Proposition 2.1 in
Gallouët et al. 2018
and Lemma 3.1 in
Goldstein and Rinott 1996
.

Lemma 13.14

(Wasserstein Gradient Bound)

If
h\in\mathcal{H}
, then the function

f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})=-\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2t}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[h\left(\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{w}+\sqrt{(1-t)}\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[h\left(\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\right]\right\}\,dt

is a solution to Stein equation (
44
) where
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{d\times d}\right)
. Moreover, we have the following bound for any first-order partial derivative,

\left|\frac{\partial f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{k}}\right|\leq\sup_{1\leq j\leq d}\left|\frac{\partial h(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right|\leq||\nabla h(\boldsymbol{w})||\leq||h||_{\text{Lip}}\leq 1,

(45)

where
||h||_{\text{Lip}}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\neq\boldsymbol{y}}\frac{|h(\boldsymbol{x})-h(\boldsymbol{y})|}{||\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}||}
.

In fact, recall Definition
13.11
, if
\boldsymbol{W}\sim p
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\boldsymbol{W}}f(\boldsymbol{W})\right]=0
for a large class of functions
f
; And the collection of all real-valued functions
f
for which
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\boldsymbol{W}}f(\boldsymbol{W})\right]=0
is called the Stein class of
T_{\boldsymbol{W}}
. From Assumption
5.3
(a), the density of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
vanishes at the boundary of its support, and we have the following Lemma
13.15
from Lemma 4 in
Fischer et al. 2022
.

Lemma 13.15

Suppose that Assumption
5.3
holds, for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}f_{h}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\right)\right]=0
, for
f_{h}
the solution to Stein equation (
44
).

We also require the following preparatory Lemma
13.16
.

Lemma 13.16

Let
||\boldsymbol{A}||_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|A_{ij}|
denote the maximum absolute row sum norm of the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. And since
-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
is positive definite, its eigenvalues are positive real numbers, with
\lambda_{\min}^{\prime}>0
the smallest eigenvalue of
-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}
. For
1\leq i,j\leq N_{\theta}
, let
a_{ij}=((-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}})_{i,j}
be the
(i,j)
-th element of the matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then for each
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, we have

\left|\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}\right|\leq\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{ju}\right|\leq||\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}||_{\infty}\leq N_{\theta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{1}{2}}.

(46)

Proof 13.17

Proof.
It follows from standard results from linear algebra, and we give its short proof as follows. The inequality
|\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}|\leq\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}|a_{ju}|
is trivial. And it is clear that
\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}|a_{ju}|\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq N_{\theta}}\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}|a_{ju}|=||(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}||_{\infty}
, so it remains to prove that
||(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}||_{\infty}\leq N_{\theta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{1}{2}}
for each
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
. Now, since
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is symmetric and positive definite with positive eigenvalues, it follows that
||(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}||=\lambda_{\max}^{\prime}
, where
\lambda_{\max}^{\prime}
is the largest eigenvalue of
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
with
\lambda_{\max}^{\prime}=\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{1}{2}}
. Also, we note the standard inequality
||\boldsymbol{A}||_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{d}||\boldsymbol{A}||
. Thus, we get the inequality (
46
).
\square

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
5.10
.

Proof 13.18

Proof.
To analyze
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{W}_{1}}|\mathbb{E}[h(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))]-\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{Z})]|
, from Lemma
13.13
, it is equivalent to analyze
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|\mathbb{E}[h(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))]-\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{Z})]|
. To do this, we use the comparison of Stein operators. By Definition
13.12
, the Stein equation for
\boldsymbol{Z}
is
T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})=h(\boldsymbol{w})-\mathbb{E}\left[h(\boldsymbol{Z})\right]
. Taking the expectation of the Stein equation with respect to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[h(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[h(\boldsymbol{Z})\right]
. From Lemma
13.15
, we have
\mathbb{E}[T_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}f_{h}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\right)]=0
. Thus, for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
, and by Definition
13.11
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}[h(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))]-\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{Z})]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}f_{h}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))-T_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}f_{h}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|T_{\boldsymbol{Z}}f_{h}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))-T_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}f_{h}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left|<\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)+\nabla\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)},\nabla f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}>\right|.

And by Equation (
41
), for all
\boldsymbol{w}
such that
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, we clearly have the PDF of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)
as follows,

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=\left(\det\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}p\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\bigg|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)\right\}.

Then for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\frac{\partial\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}}T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}\partial_{u}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}\partial_{u}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}\frac{\partial\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}}.

Now, we Taylor expand
\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}
around
\boldsymbol{w}=0
. The following formulas will be useful,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\frac{\partial^{2}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}}T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial^{2}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}\partial_{u,v}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}\partial_{u,v}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}\frac{\partial^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}\partial\theta_{v}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\frac{\partial^{3}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial^{3}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}a_{ns}\partial_{u,v,s}\eta_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}a_{ns}\partial_{u,v,s}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}a_{ns}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}\partial\theta_{v}\partial\theta_{s}}.

Recall that
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}\in
int
(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
is an equilibrium point satisfying
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}=0
. That is, from Equation (
41
),
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
solves the systems of equations for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
,

\frac{\partial\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\frac{\partial\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\partial\theta_{j}}T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-\frac{\partial\pi(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H};\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\partial\theta_{j}}=0.

(47)

Using Equation (
47
), we have
\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=0}=\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}\frac{\partial\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}=0
for
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
. In addition,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=0}=\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}\frac{\partial^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}\partial\theta_{v}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)_{jl}

\displaystyle\qquad=-\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)_{jl}=-\delta_{jl},

where
\delta_{jl}
is the Kronecker delta. Now we take the first-order Taylor expansion of
\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}
in
\boldsymbol{w}=0
using the Lagrange form of the remainder,

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=0}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=0}w_{l}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}}w_{l}w_{n}

\displaystyle=0+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}(-\delta_{jl}w_{l})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}}w_{l}w_{n}

\displaystyle=-w_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}}w_{l}w_{n},

where there exists
\alpha\in[0,1]
such that
\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}=\alpha\boldsymbol{w}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left|<\boldsymbol{w}+\nabla\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w}),\nabla f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})>\right|=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left(w_{j}+\frac{\partial\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right)\frac{\partial f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}\partial w_{l}\partial w_{n}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{w}=\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}}w_{l}w_{n}\frac{\partial f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{ju}a_{lv}a_{ns}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{u}\partial\theta_{v}\partial\theta_{s}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}w_{l}w_{n}\frac{\partial f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|\frac{\partial f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})}{\partial w_{j}}\right|\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|w_{l}w_{n}\frac{\partial^{3}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)}{\partial\theta_{j}\partial\theta_{l}\partial\theta_{n}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\left(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\sum_{u=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{ju}\right|\sum_{v=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{lv}\right|\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{ns}\right|.

(48)

From Lemma
13.14
and Lemma
13.16
, and under Assumption
5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\left|<\boldsymbol{w}+\nabla\log p_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)}(\boldsymbol{w}),\nabla f_{h}(\boldsymbol{w})>\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|w_{l}w_{n}\right|M_{1}\left(N_{\theta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}M_{1}N_{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{3}{2}}N_{\theta}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|w_{l}w_{n}\right|.

This bound holds for any
f_{h}
that solves the Stein equation with
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Using the moment bound in Assumption
5.3
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}[h(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty))]-\mathbb{E}[h(\boldsymbol{Z})]\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}M_{1}N_{\theta}^{\frac{5}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left|\tilde{\theta}_{l}(\infty)\tilde{\theta}_{n}(\infty)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}M_{1}M_{2}N_{\theta}^{\frac{9}{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{\prime-\frac{3}{2}},

which yields the desired result.
\square

13.6
Proof of Corollary
5.11

Proof 13.19

Proof.
As

\displaystyle\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)

\displaystyle=-\log\rho(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{h+1}}\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta})T_{k}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)})-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=0}^{H}A(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}))+\log\left(p(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\right\},

\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|a_{ij}\right|
for
i=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
, where
a_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-th element of the matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, are of order
(M\sum_{h=0}^{H}K_{h+1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. From Equation (
48
) in the proof of Theorem
5.10
, we infer that the bound of Theorem
5.10
is of order
(M\sum_{h=0}^{H}K_{h+1})^{-\frac{3}{2}}
(i.e.,
(M(H+1))^{-\frac{3}{2}}
related to the data size). Moreover, we consider the special case with sparse data collection shown in Equation (
4
) and the likelihood function derived from the Bayesian updating pKG-LNA metamodel, which means that the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
are Gaussian for
h=1,2,\ldots,H
and
i=1,2,\ldots,M
. From Example
13.10
, we have
K_{h+1}=|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|^{2}
, then the bound of Theorem
5.10
becomes of order
(M\sum_{h=0}^{H}(|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{t_{h+1}}|^{2}))^{-\frac{3}{2}}
.
\square

14
Proofs of Theorems in Section
6

This section provides the proofs of Lemma
6.2
, Theorem
6.4
, and Theorem
6.5
of the paper.

14.1
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Proof 14.1

Proof.
Through a Taylor expansion of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
around
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})
under
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}):=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})
, Equation (
23
) can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})-\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}-\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right\}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1}),

(49)

which can be considered as the discretized version of Equation (
15
). Following the idea of splitting Equation (
15
) into one ODE (
16
) and one SDE (
17
), we split Equation (
49
) into Equations (
50
) and (
51
),

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})

\displaystyle=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\Delta\tau,

(50)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k-1}).

(51)

For any Gaussian initial condition on
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
has a Gaussian distribution for all
k\geq 0
, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\right)
. Following the similar derivation of Proposition
4.1
, we get the discrete approximation Equations (
52
) and (
53
) to solve
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\Delta\tau,

(52)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+\left(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\right)^{\top}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})+2\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{\theta}\times N_{\theta}}\right)\Delta\tau.

(53)

Suppose the initial condition for Equation (
49
) is
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0),\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)\right)
. Leaving
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{0}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
k\geq 0
according to Equation (
52
). Then we set
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0}):=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*}(0)
. Hence, we iteratively solve two equations (
50
) and (
53
) simultaneously to get
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N}),\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})\right)
.
\square

14.2
Proof of Theorem
6.4

We follow the general ideas of the convergence analysis for SDE discretization schemes
(
Sato and Nakagawa 2014
)
. First, we review the Itô process
(
Øksendal 2003
)
, which will be used to analyze the discretization error and perform the convergence analysis for Algorithm
1
.

Definition 14.2

(Itô Process (Multidimensional))

The multidimensional stochastic process
\{\boldsymbol{x}(\tau):\tau\geq 0\}
that solves

\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{x}(0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{a}(s,\boldsymbol{x}(s))\,ds+\int_{0}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{Q}(s,\boldsymbol{x}(s))\,d\boldsymbol{W}(s)

(54)

is called the
n
-dimensional Itô process, where
\boldsymbol{a}
is an
n
-dimensional vector of nonanticipating process,
\boldsymbol{Q}
is an
n
-by-
m
matrix of Itô-integrable process, which are the drift and diffusion function respectively. And
\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
is an
m
-dimensional standard Brownian motion. Furthermore, the SDE form of the Itô process is

d\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{a}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\tau+\boldsymbol{Q}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau).

(55)

And SDE (
55
) has a unique solution if
\boldsymbol{a}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}
are Lipschitz continuous functions of linear growth.

The first integral in Equation (
54
) is an ordinary integral along the path
\boldsymbol{a}(s,\boldsymbol{x}(s))
and the second integral is the Itô stochastic integral defined by
\int_{0}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{Q}(s,\boldsymbol{x}(s))\,d\boldsymbol{W}(s)=\lim_{\Delta\tau_{\max}\to 0}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\boldsymbol{Q}(\tau_{k},\boldsymbol{x}(\tau_{k}))\Delta\boldsymbol{W}_{k}
, where we divide
[0,\tau]
into
0=\tau_{0}<\cdots<\tau_{n}=\tau
,
\Delta\tau_{\max}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n-1}\{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}\}
and
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}_{k}=\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k+1})-\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k})
. And a basic property of the Itô stochastic integral used in this paper is

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{Q}(s,\cdot)\,d\boldsymbol{W}(s)\right]=0,

(56)

where
\boldsymbol{Q}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is Itô-integrable and independent of increments
\Delta\boldsymbol{W}_{k}
.

The following Itô formula is one of the most important tools in Itô process.

Theorem 14.3

(Itô Formula (Multidimensional))

Suppose that the
n
-dimensional Itô process
\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)
satisfies the SDE
d\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{a}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\tau+\boldsymbol{Q}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
. And let
y:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable function. Then
y(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))
is also an Itô process given by

dy(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))=\frac{\partial y}{\partial\tau}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\tau+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial y}{\partial x_{i}}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))dx_{i}(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))dx_{i}(\tau)dx_{j}(\tau)

where
x_{i}(\tau)
is the
i
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)
,
i=1,2,\ldots,n
, and
dx_{i}(\tau)dx_{j}(\tau)
is computed using the rules
dW_{i}(\tau)d\tau=d\tau dW_{i}(\tau)=d\tau d\tau=0
,
dW_{i}(\tau)dW_{j}(\tau)=0
for all
i\neq j
, and
(dW_{i}(\tau))^{2}=d\tau
.

Then, we introduce the following three lemmas for Itô process, i.e., Lemma
14.4
(Grönwall inequality from Lemma 4.5.1 in
Kloeden and Platen 1992
), Lemma
14.5
(adopted from Theorem 4.5.4 in
Kloeden and Platen 1992
), and Lemma
14.6
(Feynman-Kac formula), which will be used in the proofs of Theorem
6.4
and Theorem
6.5
.

Lemma 14.4

(Grönwall Inequality)

Let
\alpha,\beta:[\tau_{0},T]\to\mathbb{R}
be integrable with
0\leq\alpha(\tau)\leq\beta(\tau)+G\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\alpha(s)\,ds
,
\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq T
, where
G>0
. Then
\alpha(\tau)\leq\beta(\tau)+G\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\exp\left\{G(\tau-s)\right\}\beta(s)\,ds
,
\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq T
.

Lemma 14.5

Suppose that
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2l}<\infty
for some integer
l\geq 1
. Then
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)||^{2l}\leq\left(1+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2l}\right)\exp\left\{D_{1}(\tau-\tau_{0})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2l}\leq D_{2}\left(1+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2l}\right)(\tau-\tau_{0})^{l}\exp\left\{D_{1}(\tau-\tau_{0})\right\},

for
\tau\in[\tau_{0},T]
where
T<\infty
,
D_{1}=2l(2l+1)C^{2}
and
D_{2}
is a positive constant depending only on
l
,
C
and
T-\tau_{0}
.

Lemma 14.6

(Feynman-Kac Formula)

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{a}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{Q}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
are smooth and bounded functions. Let
\boldsymbol{x}
be the solution of the SDE
d\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{a}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\tau+\boldsymbol{Q}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x}(\tau))d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
, and let
u(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\boldsymbol{x}(T))|\boldsymbol{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{x}\right]
. Then
u
is the solution of the Kolmogorov backward equation

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}V_{ij}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}=0,\quad\tau<T\\
u(T,\boldsymbol{x})=g(\boldsymbol{x})\end{cases},

where
\boldsymbol{V}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{Q}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{Q}^{\top}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})
and
V_{ij}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})
is the
(i,j)
-th element of
\boldsymbol{V}(\tau,\boldsymbol{x})
.

Now we are ready to give the proof of Theorem
6.4
.

Proof 14.7

Proof.
Consider the SDE of Equation (
24
), i.e.,
d\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
,
0\leq\tau\leq T
, and the SDE of Equation (
23
), i.e.,
d\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{W}(\tau)
,
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
. For theoretical use, for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, define
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
. And
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})=\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)
. Letting
\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)
for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, we have
d\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau)=\left\{\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}d\tau
. Let
\kappa_{i}(\tau)
,
a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))
and
\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
be the
i
-th components of
\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau)
,
\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
, respectively. The chain rule applied to
\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau)
shows, for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
,
d\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau)=2\kappa_{i}(\tau)\left\{a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}d\tau
. Thus,
\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})-\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k-1})=\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}2\left\{a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}\kappa_{i}(\tau)\,d\tau
. Taking the expectation of
\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k-1})\right]=\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left[2\left\{a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right]d\tau
. From the fact that
4xy\leq(x+y)^{2}\leq 2(x^{2}+y^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[2\left\{a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right\}\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left(a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))+\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right)^{2}}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right|^{2},

where

\displaystyle\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}=\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))+a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)}-\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}+R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left|\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)}-\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\right|^{2}+\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}.

By the Lipschitz condition in Assumption
6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)}-\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}\right|^{2}=\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1}))+a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\left(\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau))-a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}+\left|a_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2C_{i,1}^{2}\left(||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}+||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\right).

From Lemma
14.5
, for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\left(1+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2}\right)(\tau-\tau_{k-1})\exp\left\{D_{1}(\tau-\tau_{k-1})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\left(1+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2}\right)(\tau-\tau_{k-1})\exp\left\{D_{1}(T-\tau_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle=D_{3}(\tau-\tau_{k-1}),

where
D_{3}=D_{2}\left(1+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{0})||^{2}\right)\exp\left\{D_{1}(T-\tau_{0})\right\}
. Since
\tau_{0}=0
, the constant
D_{3}
depends only on
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
,
T
, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)
. And
