\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau_{k-1})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}=\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k-1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}2C_{i,1}^{2}\left(D_{3}(\tau-\tau_{k-1})+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right|^{2}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=C_{i,1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+2C_{i,1}^{2}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\Delta\tau+\mathbb{E}\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}\Delta\tau+\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right|^{2}\,d\tau.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\right|^{2}+2C_{i,1}^{2}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\Delta\tau+C_{i,1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}\Delta\tau+\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right|^{2}\,d\tau.

Letting
\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})=\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\right|^{2}+2C_{i,1}^{2}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\Delta\tau+C_{i,1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}\Delta\tau
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})\right]\leq\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})+\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau)\right|^{2}\,d\tau
. Then by the Grönwall inequality in Lemma
14.4
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa_{i}^{2}(\tau_{k})\right]\leq\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})+\int_{\tau_{k-1}}^{\tau_{k}}\exp(\tau_{k}-\tau)\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\,d\tau=\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\exp(\Delta\tau).

Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\right|^{2}+2C_{i,1}^{2}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\Delta\tau+C_{i,1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left|R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\right|^{2}\Delta\tau\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}+2C_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}\Delta\tau+C_{1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\Delta\tau

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2C_{1}^{2}\Delta\tau\right)\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}+C_{1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\Delta\tau,

where
C_{1}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k})||^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau_{k})\right|^{2}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left|\kappa_{i}(\tau_{k})\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\beta_{i}(\tau_{k-1})\exp(\Delta\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(1+2C_{1}^{2}\Delta\tau\right)\exp(\Delta\tau)\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}+\left(C_{1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\Delta\tau\right)\exp(\Delta\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad=\gamma\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{k-1})||^{2}+\beta(\tau_{0})\exp(\Delta\tau),

(57)

where
\gamma=\left(1+2C_{1}^{2}\Delta\tau\right)\exp(\Delta\tau)
and
\beta(\tau_{0})=C_{1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau^{2}+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\Delta\tau
. Iterating Equation (
57
) from
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{0})||^{2}=\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(0)||^{2}=\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0)||^{2}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(\tau_{N})||^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta(\tau_{0})\exp(\Delta\tau)\left(\frac{1-\gamma^{N}}{1-\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(C_{1}^{2}D_{3}\Delta\tau+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\right)\frac{\Delta\tau\exp(\Delta\tau)}{1-\gamma}\left(1-\gamma^{\frac{T}{\Delta\tau}}\right).

As
\Delta\tau\to 0
,
\gamma^{\frac{1}{\Delta\tau}}=\left(1+2C_{1}^{2}\Delta\tau\right)^{\frac{1}{\Delta\tau}}\exp\left(\Delta\tau\frac{1}{\Delta\tau}\right)\to\exp\left(1+2C_{1}^{2}\right)
. By the L’Hospital formula, we have

\lim_{\Delta\tau\to 0}\frac{\Delta\tau\exp(\Delta\tau)}{1-\gamma}=\lim_{\Delta\tau\to 0}\frac{\Delta\tau}{\exp(-\Delta\tau)-(1+2C_{1}^{2}\Delta\tau)}=\lim_{\Delta\tau\to 0}\frac{1}{-\exp(-\Delta\tau)-2C_{1}^{2}}=-\frac{1}{1+2C_{1}^{2}}.

That is, as
\Delta\tau\to 0
,
\frac{\Delta\tau\exp(\Delta\tau)}{1-\gamma}\left(1-\gamma^{\frac{T}{\Delta\tau}}\right)\to\frac{\exp\left(T+2C_{1}^{2}T\right)-1}{1+2C_{1}^{2}}
. Note that
\tau_{N}=T
, we have
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\kappa}(T)||^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\Delta\tau+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}\right)
, which leads to the conclusion
\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{\theta}(T)-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T)||=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\Delta\tau+\max_{k}\mathbb{E}||\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))||^{2}}\right)
using Jensen’s inequality.
\square

14.3
Proof of Theorem
6.5

The proof of Theorem
6.5
is given in the following.

Proof 14.8

Proof.
Let
u(\tau,\boldsymbol{\phi})=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(T))|\boldsymbol{\theta}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{\phi}\right]
,
\tau\in[0,T]
(where
0=\tau_{0}
and
T=\tau_{N}
). Note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0)
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(T))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(T))|\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)=\boldsymbol{\theta}(0)\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[u(0,\boldsymbol{\theta}(0))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[u(0,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0))\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(T))|\boldsymbol{\theta}(T)=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T)\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[u(T,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))\right].

By the Feynman-Kac formula in Lemma
14.6
,
u(\tau,\boldsymbol{\phi})
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial\phi_{i}^{2}}=0,\quad\tau<T,

\displaystyle u(T,\boldsymbol{\phi})=f(\boldsymbol{\phi}).

Then, applying the Itô formula in Theorem
14.3
to
u(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))
for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle du(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial\phi_{i}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\sqrt{2}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))dW_{i}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial\phi_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\tau}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}a_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial\phi_{i}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))dW_{i}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}(\hat{a}_{i}-a_{i})\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau+\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))dW_{i}(\tau),

where for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
and
i=1,2,\ldots,N_{\theta}
(recall that for theoretical use, for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, we define
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
),

\displaystyle a_{i}=a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)},

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{i}=\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))=\partial_{\theta_{i}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})}-R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\frac{\partial u(\tau,\boldsymbol{\phi})}{\partial\phi_{i}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\phi}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)}.

Integrating
du(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))
from
0
to
T
, we have

u(T,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))-u(0,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0))=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\int_{0}^{T}(\hat{a}_{i}-a_{i})\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\,d\tau+\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\,dW_{i}(\tau).

Taking the expectation and considering the fact that the expected value of the Itô integral is zero, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\,dW_{i}(\tau)\right]=0
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[u(T,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[u(0,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0))\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{a}_{i}-a_{i})\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\,d\tau
. Recalling that
\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))=a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{a}_{i}-a_{i})\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{a}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[(a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[(a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right].

Denote
\rho_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=(a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))-a_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau)))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))
, for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
. Then by the Itô formula in Theorem
14.3
, we have

d\rho_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\left(\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau+\sqrt{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))dW_{j}(\tau).

Since
\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))dW_{j}(\tau)\right]=\sqrt{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\mathbb{E}\left[dW_{j}(\tau)\right]=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d\rho_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))d\tau\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]d\tau.

By the boundedness theorem, there exists
M_{i,k}>0
such that for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\hat{a}_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\theta}}\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}}{\partial\phi_{j}^{2}}\right)(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\right|\leq M_{i,k}.

Thus, we obtain
\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\leq M_{i,k}(\tau_{k}-\tau_{k-1})=M_{i,k}\Delta\tau
. Similarly, denoting
\beta_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))=\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))
for
\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau<\tau_{k}
, there exists
N_{i,k}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{i}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\leq N_{i,k}\Delta\tau
. And by Equation (
40
), we have
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right]=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}){\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}}^{\top}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)+\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)\right)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\bigg|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}))\bigg|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\right]\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}){\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}}^{\top}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)+\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi_{i}}(\tau,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\tau))\right]\leq L_{i,k}N_{i,k}\Delta\tau,

where
L_{i,k}=\text{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1}){\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}}^{\top}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)+\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k-1})\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\top}(1)\right)
. Using
C_{i,\max}=\max_{k}\left\{M_{i,k}+L_{i,k}N_{i,k}\right\}
and
C_{\max}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}C_{i,\max}
, we get the conclusion

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}(T))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left[u(T,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(T))\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[u(0,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(0))\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\theta}}\int_{0}^{T}C_{i,\max}\Delta\tau d\tau=TC_{\max}\Delta\tau=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau),

\square

15
RAPTOR-GEN as a Warm Start

We can employ the Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm (MALA) as a “corrector” after executing Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
, namely, to use RAPTOR-GEN as a warm start for MALA.

MALA combines discretized LD with a Metropolis-Hastings step to correct the bias in the stationary distribution induced by discretization. Specifically, based on ULA, we consider the update rule (
13
) and define a proposal distribution to generate a new MCMC posterior sample
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
,

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\big|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\big|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})}\Delta\tau+\sqrt{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{W}(\tau_{k}).

(58)

Then, the candidate samples
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
generated from this proposal are accepted with the ratio,

\gamma_{\rm acc}:=\min\left\{1,\frac{p\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)q_{\rm trans}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\bigg|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})\right)}{p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)q_{\rm trans}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})\bigg|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})\right)}\right\},

(59)

where the proposal distribution
q_{\rm trans}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\propto\exp\{-\frac{1}{4\Delta\tau}||\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\Delta\tau||^{2}\}
is the transition probability density from
\boldsymbol{\theta}
to
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
obtained from Equation (
58
).

In MALA, the selection of starting points (a.k.a. warm start) is a critical task. Compared to discretized LD, in addition to computing the gradient of the log-posterior once, each iteration of MALA also requires the evaluation of the posterior distribution once in each Metropolis-Hastings step. Selecting the starting point of MALA cleverly helps improve its mixing, thus saving computational budget. It has been shown in
Chewi et al. 2021
that the mixing time of MALA for sampling from a standard Gaussian target distribution of parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
with a warm start is at least
\mathcal{O}(N_{\theta}^{\frac{1}{3}})
. Intuitively, to attain this best achievable mixing time, we need to find an initial distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
that is close to its target distribution. It is a natural choice to use LD-LNA in RAPTOR-GEN as initialization, as the finite-sample and asymptotic performance of the proposed LD-LNA shown in Section
5.3
demonstrate the closeness between LD-LNA and the target posterior distribution.

We provide a detailed MALA procedure with RAPTOR-GEN as initialization in Algorithm
5
to generate posterior samples for parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
when the parameter dimension is high or the data size is small. In particular, within each iteration of the MALA posterior sampler, given the previous sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
, we compute and generate one proposal sample
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
from the discretized LD (
58
), and accept it with the Metropolis-Hastings ratio (
59
). Repetition of this procedure can get samples
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta+1
(we still remove the first
N_{0}
samples and keep one for every
\delta
samples to reduce initial bias and correlations between consecutive samples). Consequently, we obtain the posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
. Still, if the support of parameters is not real space such as positive space, we need to transform the parameters from their original support to real space using the reparameterization mentioned in Remark
5.2
, and then perform Algorithm
5
on the transformed parameters.

Input:

The prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, historical observation dataset
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}
, step size
\Delta\tau
, posterior sample size
B
, initial warm-up length
N_{0}
, and an appropriate integer
\delta
to reduce sample correlation.

Output:

Posterior samples
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})
.

1.
Run Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
and set the starting point
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
as one of the approximate posterior samples obtained by Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
;

for

k=0,1,\ldots,N_{0}+(B-1)\delta

do

2.
Generate a proposal
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
through Equation (
58
);

3.
Calculate the acceptance ratio
\gamma_{\rm acc}
through Equation (
59
);

4.
Draw
u
from the continuous uniform distribution on the interval
[0,1]
;

if

u\leq\gamma_{\rm acc}

then

5.
The proposal
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
is accepted, and set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1}):=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
;
else if

u>\gamma_{\rm acc}

then

6.
The proposal
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})
is rejected, and set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1}):=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})
;
7.
Return
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(b)}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{0}+(b-1)\delta+1})
for
b=1,2,\ldots,B
.

Algorithm 5

MALA posterior sampler with RAPTOR-GEN as a warm start for parameters of SRN.

As a support to the above MALA initialization scheme, suppose that certain conditions are satisfied, such as the localized (rescaled) target posterior to be close to a Gaussian distribution (the proposed LD-LNA satisfies this condition as shown in the finite-sample and asymptotic performance in Section
5.3
), an upper bound for the corresponding warming parameter under the Gaussian initial distribution constrained on a compact set can be provided, which means that the warming parameter can be controlled; see a recent work
Tang and Yang 2024
for a more rigorous statement.

Compared to existing theoretical work such as
Tang and Yang 2024
, Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
for the implementation of RAPTOR-GEN provides a warm start that is easy to implement for MALA. Since Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
helps to select an initial distribution that well approximates the target posterior distribution as shown in Section
6.2
, the mixing time of MALA would be short; thus much fewer iterations of MALA have to be performed to obtain better posterior samples than the one without such a warm start.

Recall the results of Theorem
5.10
, that is, when the largest eigenvalue of the matrix
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
(i.e.,
\lambda_{\max}
) is smaller, the LD-LNA better approximates the true posterior distribution. Proposition
15.1
provides theoretical guidance on the necessity of an MALA after the execution of Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
. In practice, for Algorithm
1
or Algorithm
2
, the respective largest eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N})
or
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{*}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{N_{1}+N_{2}})
can serve as a criterion to assess its performance, and the supplementary MALA is only required when this criterion is relatively significant.

Proposition 15.1

Suppose that Assumption
5.3
holds. Under the exponential family assumption of the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h+1}}^{(i)}|\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{1}:t_{h}}^{(i)};\boldsymbol{\theta}
for
i=1,\ldots,M
,
h=1,\ldots,H
, fix a tolerance
\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}>0
, then the LD-LNA with covariance
(-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}\log p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H})|_{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}})^{-1}
whose largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\max}\leq C^{-\frac{2}{3}}N_{\theta}^{-3}\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}^{\frac{2}{3}}
, where
C=\frac{1}{2}M_{1}M_{2}
, has a 1-Wasserstein distance between
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\theta}}
as in Equation (
20
) and
N_{\theta}
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{Z}
restricted by
d_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\infty),\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\leq\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}
.

16
Supplements to Empirical Study

Throughout the empirical study, the initial value
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{0})
of the ULA and the initial value
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(0)
of the one- and two-stage RAPTOR-GEN algorithms (i.e., Algorithms
1
and
2
) are sampled from specified priors
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

16.1
Lotka-Volterra Model

To assess the empirical performance of the proposed RAPTOR-GEN in scenarios with dense data collection, we use a toy example of SRN, i.e., the Lotka-Volterra model
(
Wilkinson 2018
)
, involving a population of two competing species, i.e., Prey and Predator. It describes predators that die with rate
v_{3}
and reproduce with rate
v_{2}
by consuming prey that reproduce with rate
v_{1}
, represented by the following three reaction equations,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Reaction }1:\ {\rm Prey}\xrightarrow{v_{1}}2{\rm Prey}\quad\text{(Prey reproduction)},\\
\text{Reaction }2:\ {\rm Prey}+{\rm Predator}\xrightarrow{v_{2}}2{\rm Predator}\quad\text{(Predator reproduction)},\\
\text{Reaction }3:\ {\rm Predator}\xrightarrow{v_{3}}\emptyset\quad\text{(Predator death)},\end{array}

\displaystyle~~~\mbox{with}~~~\boldsymbol{C}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&0\\
0&1&-1\end{pmatrix}.

The system state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=(s_{t}^{1},s_{t}^{2})^{\top}
includes the respective number of prey and predators at any time
t
. We use mass-action kinetics to model reaction rates as
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})=(\theta_{1}s_{t}^{1},\theta_{2}s_{t}^{1}s_{t}^{2},\theta_{3}s_{t}^{2})^{\top}
. Our goal is to infer the unknown kinetic rate parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\right)^{\top}
.

We simulate one measurement trajectory (i.e.,
M=1
) in the time interval
[t_{1},t_{H+1}]
using the Gillespie algorithm with the true parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\rm true}=\left(0.5,0.0025,0.3\right)^{\top}
and the initial state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t_{1}}=\left(71,79\right)^{\top}
. Here, we consider dense data collection and use the state transition density (
3
) for the likelihood function. From Example
3.3
in Section
3.2
, to make the likelihood approximation error sufficiently small, suppose that we have observations at evenly spaced times
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{H+1}
with
\Delta t=0.1
. We assess the performance of RAPTOR-GEN under model uncertainty induced by different data sizes, i.e.,
H=10,20,30,40,50
. We take ULA as a benchmark. To ensure that the discretization error of ULA is sufficiently small so that we can take the samples generated by ULA as real posterior samples of
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
, we set
c=0.1
to make the step size sufficiently small. And we set the initial warm-up length
N_{0}=10000
to ensure convergence and
\delta=10
to reduce the sample correlation for ULA; set the tolerances
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=1\times 10^{-6}
for two-stage RAPTOR-GEN; the posterior sample size
B=1000
for both algorithms. The priors of the parameters are set as
\theta_{1}\sim U(0,5)
,
\theta_{2}\sim U(0,0.5)
, and
\theta_{3}\sim U(0,5)
.

Figure
16.1
compares the approximate posterior distributions obtained by the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN and ULA with five data sizes for three log-parameters. From their overall shapes, the posterior distributions obtained by two-stage RAPTOR-GEN well approximate those obtained by ULA, and converge to the true values as the data size increases. In particular, for two-stage RAPTOR-GEN, the uncertainty in the model parameter estimates decreases substantially with increasing data size, which is consistent with the estimates of
\lambda_{\max}
in Table
16.1
, decreasing as the data size increases; for ULA, the long tails of some distributions indicate its instability. Table
16.1
also summarizes
N_{1}
, i.e., the iteration step in which condition
||\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k+1})-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\Delta\tau}(\tau_{k})||\leq\varepsilon_{1}
is satisfied in Stage 1 of the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN, which decreases significantly with increasing data size and is less than
N_{0}=10000
used by ULA at all sample sizes.

\log(\theta_{1})
.

.

.

16.2
ABC-type Samplers in Section
7.1

This section provides the detailed procedures for two ABC-type samplers, i.e., the ABC rejection sampler and the ABC-SMC sampler
(
Warne et al. 2019
)
, and the results of parameter inference by the ABC-SMC sampler.

The basis of ABC is a discrepancy metric
d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)
that provides a measure of closeness between observational data
\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}=\{\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)}\}_{h=1}^{H+1}\}_{i=1}^{M}
collected from experiments and simulated data
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}=\{\{\boldsymbol{x}_{t_{h}}^{(i)}\}_{h=1}^{H+1}\}_{i=1}^{M}
generated by stochastic simulation of biochemical SRN with simulated measurement error. Thus, it places an acceptance condition
d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)\leq\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is the discrepancy threshold. This yields an approximation to Bayes’ rule,

p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)\approx p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}|d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)\leq\epsilon\right)=\frac{p\left(d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)\leq\epsilon|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{p\left(d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)\leq\epsilon\right)}.

(60)

The key insight here is that the ability to produce sample paths of a biochemical SRN model (i.e., the forwards problem) enables an approximate algorithm for inference (i.e., the inverse problem), regardless of the intractability or complexity of the likelihood. That is, the closed form of likelihood needs not even be known. In this paper, the discrepancy metric used is
d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}\right)=\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{h=1}^{H+1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{t_{h}}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{x}_{t_{h}}^{(i)})^{2}\}^{1/2}
, where the simulated data
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}=\{\{\boldsymbol{x}_{t_{h}}^{(i)}\}_{h=1}^{H+1}\}_{i=1}^{M}
are generated using the Gillespie algorithm
(
Gillespie 1977
)
, and we denote
p\left(\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
as the process of generating the simulated data given a parameter vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
that is identical to the processes used to generate our synthetic observational data. Furthermore, as the dimensionality of the data increases, small values of
\epsilon
are not computationally feasible. In such cases, the data dimensionality may be reduced by computing lower dimensional summary statistics; however, these must be sufficient statistics to ensure that the ABC posterior still converges to the correct posterior as
\epsilon\to 0
. We refer the readers to
Fearnhead and Prangle 2012
for more details on this topic.

16.2.1
ABC Rejection Sampler

ABC rejection sampler is an essential computational method for generating
B
samples
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon}^{(2)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon}^{(B)}
from the ABC posterior equation (
60
) with
\epsilon>0
. Its detailed procedure is summarized as follows.

1.

Initialize index
b=0
;

2.

Generate a prior sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\sim p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
;

3.

Generate simulated data
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H*}\sim p\left(\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\right)
;

4.

If
d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H*}\right)\leq\epsilon
, accept
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon}^{(b+1)}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
and set
b:=b+1
, otherwise continue;

5.

If
b=B
, terminate sampling, otherwise go to Step 2.

16.2.2
ABC-SMC Sampler

For small
\epsilon
, the computational burden of the ABC rejection sampler can be prohibitive since the acceptance rate is very low, which is especially an issue for biochemical SRNs with highly variable dynamics.
Sisson et al. 2007
provides a solution via an ABC variant of SMC. The fundamental idea is to use sequential importance resampling to propagate
B
samples, called particles, through a sequence of
P+1
ABC posterior distributions defined through a sequence of discrepancy thresholds
\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{P}
with
\epsilon_{p}>\epsilon_{p+1}
for
p=0,1,\ldots,P-1
and
\epsilon_{0}=\infty
(that is,
p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{0}}|\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H}\right)
is the prior). This results in the ABC-SMC sampler as follows.

1.

Initialize
p=0
and weights
\omega_{p}^{(b)}=1/B
for
b=1,2,\ldots,B
;

2.

Generate
B
particles from the prior
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(b)}\sim p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
for
1,2,\ldots,B
;

3.

Set index
b=0
;

4.

Randomly select integer
j
from the set
\{1,2,\ldots,B\}
with
\mathbb{P}\left(j=1\right)=\omega_{p}^{(1)},\mathbb{P}\left(j=2\right)=\omega_{p}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbb{P}\left(j=B\right)=\omega_{p}^{(B)}
;

5.

Generate proposal
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(j)})
;

6.

Generate simulated data
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H*}\sim p\left(\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H}|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\right)
;

7.

If
d\left(\mathcal{D}_{M}^{H},\mathcal{S}_{M}^{H*}\right)>\epsilon_{p+1}
, go to Step 4, otherwise continue;

8.

Set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p+1}}^{(b+1)*}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
,
\omega_{p+1}^{(b+1)*}:=p\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\right)/\sum_{j=1}^{B}\omega_{p}^{(j)}q(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(j)})
, and
b:=b+1
;

9.

If
b<B
, go to Step 4, otherwise continue;

10.

Set index
b=0
;

11.

Randomly select integer
j
from the set
\{1,2,\ldots,B\}
with
\mathbb{P}\left(j=1\right)=\omega_{p+1}^{(1)*},\mathbb{P}\left(j=2\right)=\omega_{p+1}^{(2)*},\ldots,\mathbb{P}\left(j=B\right)=\omega_{p+1}^{(B)*}
;

12.

Set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p+1}}^{(b+1)}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p+1}}^{(j)*}
and
b:=b+1
;

13.

If
b<B
, go to Step 11, otherwise continue;

14.

Set
\omega_{p+1}^{(b)}:=1/\sum_{j=1}^{B}\omega_{p+1}^{(j)*}
for
b=1,2,\ldots,B
and
p:=p+1
;

15.

If
p<P
, go to Step 3, otherwise terminate sampling.

Here,
q(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(j)})
is the proposal kernel, whose choice is nontrivial. For the ABC-SMC sampler in Section
7.1
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}:=(\theta_{3},\sigma)^{\top}
, and we set the priors
p\left(\theta_{3}\right):=U(0,1)
and
p\left(\sigma\right):=U(0,25)
,
q(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(j)}):=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\epsilon_{p}}^{(j)},\text{diag}\left((0.05(1-0))^{2},(0.05(25-0))^{2}\right))
, the number of particles
B:=100
, and a sequence of discrepancy acceptance thresholds
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{P}
with
P:=5
is
[80,40,20,10,5]
,
[100,50,25,12.5,6.25]
, and
[120,60,30,15,7.5]
for three data sizes
H=4,8,16
, respectively.

The results of parameter inference using the ABC-SMC sampler are presented in Table
16.2.2
. ABC-type methods rely on exact simulation to generate synthetic data given a set of parameters, and then assess the similarity between the simulated and observed data to determine parameter acceptance—without requiring explicit likelihood evaluation. As a result, ABC-SMC avoids likelihood approximation errors, which leads to lower parameter RMSEs when the dataset is extremely small (e.g.,
H=4
), compared to the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN. However, this comes at the cost of significantly higher computational time.
A comparison of Tables
7.1
and
16.2.2
further highlights the advantages of RAPTOR-GEN. With a moderate number of observations (e.g.,
H=8
and
16
), the likelihood approximation error in RAPTOR-GEN diminishes, resulting in lower parameter RMSEs than those of ABC-SMC. By fully exploiting the closed-form likelihood available from a moderately sized dataset, the two-stage RAPTOR-GEN achieves substantially faster convergence. In contrast, ABC-type samplers often require millions of simulated sample paths, making them computationally impractical when the data generation process is expensive.
